Stationary phase with Cauchy singularity. A critical point of signature ( + , − ) (+,-)
Christian Klein, Johannes Sjöstrand, Maher Zerzeri

Abstract




Abstract
:
Asymptotic expressions for an integral appearing in the
solution of a d-bar problem are presented. The integral is a solid
Cauchy transform of a function with a rapidly oscillating phase with
a small parameter
h
,
0<h\ll 1
.
Whereas standard steepest descent approaches can be applied to the
case where the stationary points of the phase
\omega_{k}
,

k=1,\ldots,N
are far from the
singularity
\zeta
of the integrand, a polarization approach is proposed for
the case that
|\zeta-\omega_{k}|<\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{h})
for some
k
. In this
case the problem is studied in
\mathbb{C}^{2}

(
\tilde{\omega}:=\bar{\omega}
is treated as an independent variable)
on steepest descent contours. An application of Stokes’ theorem allows
for a decomposition of the integral into three terms for which
asymptotics expressions in terms of special functions are given.

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification.–
26B20, 45E05, 41A60, 30E20, 30E15.

Key words and phrases.–
Stokes’ formula, polarization, deep descent method, stationary phase approximation.

This work is partially supported by
the ANR project ISAAC-ANR-23-CE40-0015-01, the EIPHI Graduate
School (contract ANR-17-EURE-0002) and the region
Bourgogne-Franche-Comté

1
Introduction

This paper is devoted to the asymptotic behaviour for small
h
of the integral:

I(a,\phi,\zeta;h)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega;h)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega)}\,\,{L(\mathrm{d}\omega)}\,,\quad h\rightarrow 0\,,

(1.1)

where
\displaystyle{L(\mathrm{d}\omega)}=\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Re}(\omega)\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Im}(\omega)=\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}
is the Lebesgue measure on the complex plane. Here

\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
,
a,\phi
are real-analytic in
neigh
(\zeta;\mathbb{C})
1
1

1

Let
M
be a topological space. Let
N
be a subset of
M
. The set neigh(
N,M
) denotes
some neighborhood of
N
in
M
.
. The function
a

is assumed to be a symbol, the function
\phi
to have a finite
number of stationary points
\omega_{k}
,
k=1,\ldots,N
with
non-degenerate Hessian.

Such integrals appear in the solution of d-bar problems since the solid Cauchy transform
(
1.1
) provides the solution of a d-bar equation,

\frac{\partial I}{\partial\overline{\zeta}}(a,\phi,\zeta;h)=a(\zeta,\overline{\zeta};h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\zeta,\overline{\zeta})}\,.

The d-bar problems appear in the theory of integrable equations in
2D as the Davey-Stewartson (DS) equation, see e.g.
[
KlSa21_01
]
, and in

electrical impedance tomography
(EIT), see

[
4
,
MuSi12_01
,
16
,
20
]
, and for Normal
Matrix Models in Random Matrix Theory, see e.g.
[
11
]
.
Since the integrals of the
form (
1.1
) are considered for all values of

\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
, they can be seen as a nonlinear variant of the
Fourier transform.

For general functions
a
,
\phi
, the integral (
1.1
) can
only be computed numerically. Because of the importance of d-bar
problems in applications there are many
numerical approaches to solve them. The standard method is to use
Fourier techniques, i.e. to compute the integral after a Fourier
transform in
\zeta
by approximating the transform via a discrete
Fourier transform in standard manner, see
[
15
,
MuSi12_01
]
for a
first order method.
The first Fourier approach with an exponential decrease of the
numerical error with the number of Fourier modes, or
spectral
convergence
, was presented in
[
10
,
9
,
11
]
for Schwartz class potentials via an analytical regularization of the integrand in the solid Cauchy
transform. A spectral approach for potentials with compact support on
a disk was presented in
[
14
]
, a problem important in EIT applications.
However this approach cannot be
applied for all values of
h
since the solution becomes too
oscillatory for small
h
to be computed numerically. Thus
asymptotic formulae for small
h
were presented in

[
13
,
12
]
for functions
a
with compact support on
domains with a strictly convex smooth boundary. This allowed for a
hybrid approach, the numerical computation for
h>h_{min}
and
the asymptotic expressions
[
13
,
12
]
for
h<h_{min}

with some suitably chosen
h_{min}
such that in both domains the
numerical respectively asymptotic expressions are of similar accuracy.

In the present paper we aim at the first step in extending the
approach of
[
13
,
12
]
to more general functions
a
,
for instance those appearing in
the asymptotic description of the
reflection coefficient
in
the DS case, see Subsection
1.1
. The basic idea is to apply
a standard stationary phase approximation in case
\zeta
is not
close to any of the stationary points,

|\zeta-\omega_{k}|>\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{h})
for all
k=1,\ldots,N
.
If
|\zeta-\omega_{k}|<\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{h})
for some

k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, a
polarization approach
is applied. This
means that
\overline{\omega}
in (
1.1
) is treated as an
independent variable
\widetilde{\omega}
, i.e. the integral
(
1.1
) is considered in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. After an
application of Stokes’ theorem, suitable contours for the
integration are introduced in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
via steepest descent
arguments. This allows to express the integrals in this case via
transcendental functions in leading order as in
[
KlSjSt21_01
]
.

1.1
Background and motivation

One application of d-bar equations is in the inverse scattering
approach to completely integrable equations in 2D as
the Davey-Stewartson II (DS II) equation

\begin{split}\mathrm{i}q_{t}+(q_{xx}-q_{yy})+2\sigma(\Phi+|q|^{2})q&=0\\
\Phi_{xx}+\Phi_{yy}+2(|q|^{2})_{xx}&=0\,,\end{split}

(1.2)

where indices denote partial derivatives, and
q=q(x,y)
is a
complex-valued field. Thus equation (
1.2
) is a two-dimensional nonlinear Schrödinger equation; the equation is
defocusing

for
\sigma=1
, and
focusing
for
\sigma=-1
. Here
\Phi
denotes a mean field. For further examples as the Kadomtsev-Petviashvili and the Novikov-Veselov
equation, see
[
KlSa21_01
]
for many references.

Both the scattering and the inverse scattering problem for the DS
II equation are given by the Dirac system

\begin{cases}\overline{\partial}\upphi_{1}=\frac{1}{2}q\mathrm{e}^{\overline{k}\overline{z}-kz}\upphi_{2}\\
\partial\upphi_{2}=\sigma\frac{1}{2}\overline{q}\mathrm{e}^{kz-\overline{k}\overline{z}}\upphi_{1}\,,\qquad\sigma=\pm 1\,,\end{cases}

(1.3)

subject to the asymptotic conditions

\lim_{|z|\to+\infty}\upphi_{1}(z)=1\,,\qquad\lim_{|z|\to+\infty}\upphi_{2}(z)=0\,,

(1.4)

where the
spectral parameter

k\in\mathbb{C}
is independent of
z=x+\mathrm{i}y
,

(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
where

\partial:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x}-\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{\partial}:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\right)\,.

The system (
1.3
) appears also in the context of Calderón’s problem, see
[
5
]
.
The scattering data for DS, the so-called
reflection
coefficient

R
are defined by

\overline{R}=\frac{2\sigma}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{e}^{kz-\overline{k}\overline{z}}\,\,\overline{q}(z)\,\upphi_{1}(z;k)\,\,L(\mathrm{d}z)\,.

(1.5)

In
[
13
,
12
]
asymptotic formulae for large
|k|

(small
h:=|k|^{-1}
) were
derived for the reflection coefficient for the characteristic
function of a compact domain with smooth strictly convex boundary.
In the case of the unit disk one gets

R\approx\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi|k|^{3}}}\left(\sin\Big(2|k|-\frac{\pi}{4}\Big)-\frac{5}{16|k|}\cos\Big(2|k|-\frac{\pi}{4}\Big)\right)\,,

(1.6)

see
[
12
, Subsection 4.4, page 4935]
, and
[
13
, Subsection 6.3]
for more details.

The reflection coefficient evolves in time by a trivial phase factor:

R(k;t)=R(k;0)\mathrm{e}^{4\mathrm{i}t\mathrm{Re}(k^{2})}\,.

(1.7)

The inverse scattering transform for DS II, i.e. the reconstruction
of
q(z,t)
for given
R(k;t)
is again given by
(
1.3
) and
(
1.4
) after the exchange of
k
and
z
and
q
and
R

respectively. Since the system (
1.3
)
can be written with (
1.4
) in the form

\displaystyle\upphi_{1}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{q}{\omega-z}\exp(\overline{k}\,\overline{\omega}-k\,\omega)\upphi_{2}\,{L(\mathrm{d}\omega)}

\displaystyle\upphi_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\bar{q}}{\bar{\omega}-\bar{z}}\exp(k\,\omega-\overline{k}\,\overline{\omega})\upphi_{1}\,{L(\mathrm{d}\omega)}\,,

(1.8)

it can be solved iteratively as in
[
13
]
with the
lowest order terms being
\upphi_{1}^{0}=1
,
\upphi_{2}^{0}=0
. This
leads to the lowest order non-trivial contribution to
\upphi_{2}

\upphi_{2}^{1}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\bar{q}}{\bar{\omega}-\bar{z}}\exp(k\,\omega-\overline{k}\,\overline{\omega})\,{L(\mathrm{d}\omega)}\,.

(1.9)

For the example that
\bar{q}
is given by the asymptotic expression
(
1.6
) and (
1.7
), we can write
\upphi_{2}^{1}=\bar{I}_{+}+\bar{I}_{-}
, where
I_{\pm}
are in leading order integrals of the form
(
1.1
) with

a_{\pm}=\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi|\omega|^{3}}}\,,\qquad\phi_{\pm}=2\tau(\omega^{2}+\bar{\omega}^{2})+\frac{k\omega-\bar{k}\bar{\omega}}{\mathrm{i}}\pm 2\sqrt{\omega\bar{\omega}}\,.

Thus it will be important to obtain asymptotic expression for
integrals of the form (
1.1
) for small
h
for functions
a

and
\phi
including the above examples.

1.2
Main results of the paper

We assume in this paper that the phase
\phi
in
(
1.1
) has a finite number
N
of stationary points
\omega_{k}
for

k=1,\ldots,N
with non-degenerate Hessian. This allows in the
following to concentrate on the case of a single such stationary
point that we can assume without loss of generality at the origin. In
this paper we concentrate on the case of a Hessian at this point with
signature
(+,-)
.

Let
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\xi,\eta}\simeq\mathbb{C}_{\omega}
,
\omega=\xi+\mathrm{i}\eta
be an open neighborhood of
0
, let

\phi\in C^{\infty}(V;\mathbb{R})
satisfy (
2.1
)–(
2.3
), ı.e.

\phi(0)=0,\qquad\phi^{\prime}(0)=0\,,

{\rm det}\big(\phi^{\prime\prime}(0)\big)\not=0\,,

\phi^{\prime}(\omega)\not=0,\hbox{ when }\,\omega\in V\setminus\{0\}\,.

We are interested in the asymptotic behaviour of the expression (
2.4
)

\begin{split}I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)&=\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int\frac{1}{\zeta-(\xi+\mathrm{i}\eta)}\,a(\xi,\eta;h)\chi(\xi,\eta)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\xi,\eta)}d\xi d\eta\\
&=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega;h)\chi(\omega)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega)}\,L(\mathrm{d}\omega)\,,\end{split}

where
a(\xi,\eta;h)=a(\omega;h)\in C^{\infty}(V\times]0,h_{0}])
is a classical symbol
of order
h^{0}
, i.e. such that we have (
2.5
):

a(\omega;h)\sim a_{0}(\omega)+ha_{1}(\omega)+\ldots\hbox{ in
}C^{\infty}(V)\,,\quad h\longrightarrow 0\,,

and
\chi(\omega)\in C_{0}^{\infty}(V)
is a fixed cutoff function, equal
to 1 near
\omega=0
. Here we also assume that
\zeta\in\mathrm{int}(\chi^{-1}(1))
.

The asymptotic behaviour is generated in a small neighborhood of

\{0,\zeta\}
, where
0
is the critical point of
\phi(\omega)

and
\zeta
the locus of the pole singularity of the integrand. When

|\zeta|\geq 1/{\mathcal{O}}(1)
2
2

2

Here we follow the convention that the expression “
\mathcal{O}(1)
” in a denominator denotes a
bounded positive quantity.
, we can decompose the integral with
a partition of unity and study the contribution from
\omega=0
with
the stationary phase method and the one from
\omega=\zeta
,
observing that the corresponding contribution is of the form
c(\zeta;h)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi(\zeta)/h}
, where
c(\zeta;h)\sim h(c_{1}(\omega)+hc_{2}(\omega)+...)
solves asymptotically

\partial_{\overline{\omega}}\Big(c\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\phi}{h}}\Big)=\big(a(\omega;h)+{\mathcal{O}}(h^{\infty})\big)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\phi}{h}}\,,\quad\omega\in\mathrm{neigh}(\zeta;\mathbb{C})\,,

by means of an iterated division procedure, starting with
c_{1}(\omega)\mathrm{i}\partial_{\overline{\omega}}\phi(\omega)=a_{0}(\omega)
.
Here
f(h)=\mathcal{O}(h^{\infty})
means that for all integer
N\in\mathbb{N}
there exist
C_{N}>0
such that
|f(h)|\leq C_{N}h^{N}
,
\forall h\in]0,h_{0}]
.
This leads to (
2.16
):

I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)=c(\zeta;h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\zeta)}+b(\zeta;h)+\mathcal{O}(h^{\infty})\,,

where the symbols
b
,
c
are order
h
and satisfy (
2.11
), (
2.14
).

In the following we study the case when
|\zeta|\ll 1
. Put

\varepsilon:=\frac{|\zeta|}{\sqrt{h}}

(1.10)

and assume first that
\varepsilon
satisfies (
2.6
), i.e.

\varepsilon\gg 1\,.

Write
\zeta=\lambda\widetilde{\zeta}
, where
\lambda\asymp|\zeta|
is an extra parameter, and define
\widetilde{\omega}
by

\omega=\lambda\widetilde{\omega}
.
Write as in (
2.23
),

\frac{1}{h}\phi(\omega)=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{h}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi(\lambda\widetilde{\omega})=\frac{1}{\widetilde{h}}\widetilde{\phi}(\widetilde{\omega})\,,

where as in (
2.24
)

\widetilde{h}=\frac{h}{\lambda^{2}}\asymp\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\ll 1,\qquad\widetilde{\phi}(\widetilde{\omega})=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi(\lambda\widetilde{\omega})\,.

Making the same changes of variables in (
2.16
), we will get
(
2.27
):

I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)=\mathcal{O}\left(\widetilde{h}^{\infty}\right)+\lambda\left(\widetilde{c}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\widetilde{h}}\tilde{\phi}(\widetilde{\omega})}+\widetilde{b}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})+\mathcal{O}\big(\widetilde{h}^{\infty}\big)\right)\,,

where
\widetilde{c}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h}),\widetilde{b}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})
are classical symbols of order
\widetilde{h}
, satisfying the analogues of (
2.13
), (
2.10
):

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\widetilde{c}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})\sim\widetilde{h}\big(\widetilde{c}_{1}(\widetilde{\zeta})+\widetilde{h}\widetilde{c}_{2}(\widetilde{\zeta})+\ldots\,\big)\,,\\
\\
\widetilde{b}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})\sim\widetilde{h}\big(\widetilde{b}_{1}(\widetilde{\zeta})+\widetilde{h}\widetilde{b}_{2}(\widetilde{\zeta})+\ldots\,\big)\,.\end{array}\right.

On the formal asymptotics level we have (
2.31
):

\lambda\widetilde{c}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})=c(\zeta;h)\,,\qquad\lambda\widetilde{b}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})=b(\zeta;h)\,.

See Section
2
, in particular Proposition
2.1
.

The main part of this work is about the case when

\varepsilon\leq h^{-\delta}

(1.11)

for some fixed
\delta\in]0,1/6[
. We then need to go out
to the complex domain and assume that
a(\xi,\eta;h)
and
\phi(\xi,\eta)
extend to
holomorphic functions in a neighborhood of
0
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\xi,\eta}
that is independent of
h
. We assume that

\mathrm{supp}\chi
is contained in that neighborhood.

We will use the method of polarization and start by
identifying
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\xi,\eta}
with the antidiagonal in
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\omega,\widetilde{\omega}}
as in (
3.12
),

\mathrm{adiag\,}(\mathbb{C}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}^{2})=\{(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}};\,\,\,\widetilde{\omega}=\overline{\omega}\}\,.

Here we put as in (
3.13
)

\omega=\xi+\mathrm{i}\eta\,,\hbox{ and also }\widetilde{\omega}=\xi-\mathrm{i}\eta\,.

Since

\mathrm{d}\xi\wedge\mathrm{d}\eta=\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\,,

the integral in (
3.11
) can be written as in (
3.14
)

I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathrm{adiag\,}({\mathbb{C}}^{2})}\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\,,

abusing slightly the notation, letting
a
,
\chi
,
\phi
denote
“the same” functions on
\mathrm{adiag\,}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
using
(
3.13
) and the inverse relations (
3.15
)

\xi=\frac{\omega+\widetilde{\omega}}{2}\,,\quad\eta=\frac{\omega-\widetilde{\omega}}{2\mathrm{i}}\,.

The idea is then to deform the contour

\mathrm{adiag}({\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}})
to a new contour in

{\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}
which is a Cartesian product

\gamma_{1}\times\gamma_{2}
, where
\gamma_{1}\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}
and

\gamma_{2}\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{\tilde{\omega}}
are curves. Stokes’ formula
will then produce several terms to be analyzed, in particular the
integral along
\gamma_{1}\times\gamma_{2}
which reduces to 1D
integrals (see (
3.16
)):

\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\gamma_{1}\times\gamma_{2}}(...)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega=\int_{\gamma_{1}}\left(\int_{\gamma_{2}}(...)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\right)\mathrm{d}\omega\,.

We define a smooth family of contours
\Gamma_{\theta}
,
\theta\in[0,1]
, of real dimension 2 in
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\xi,\eta}
or equivalently in
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}
. In the
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\xi,\eta}
representation it is
given by

\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(t,s)\longmapsto(\xi(\theta,t,s),\eta(\theta,t,s))\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\,,

where as in (
3.18
)

\begin{cases}\xi(\theta;t,s)=(1+\mathrm{i}\theta)t\\
\eta(\theta;t,s)=(1-\mathrm{i}\theta)s\,,\\
\end{cases}\quad(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,.

In the
{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega,\widetilde{\omega}}
representation it is
given as in (
3.19
) by

\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(t,s)\,\longmapsto f(\theta;t,s)=\big(\omega(\theta;t,s)\,,\,\widetilde{\omega}(\theta;t,s)\big)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\,,

where as in (
3.20
)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega(\theta;t,s)&:=&\xi(\theta;t,s)+\mathrm{i}\eta(\theta;t,s)\\
&=&(t+\mathrm{i}s)+\mathrm{i}\theta(t-\mathrm{i}s)\\
\widetilde{\omega}(\theta;t,s)&:=&\xi(\theta;t,s)-\mathrm{i}\eta(\theta;t,s)\\
&=&(t-\mathrm{i}s)+\mathrm{i}\theta(t+\mathrm{i}s)\\
\end{array}\right.,\quad(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,.

In the
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\xi,\eta}
representation, we have
\Gamma_{\theta}=(1+\mathrm{i}\theta)\mathbb{R}\times(1-\mathrm{i}\theta)\mathbb{R}
, especially
\Gamma_{0}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
In the
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}
representation, we
get
\Gamma_{0}=\mathrm{adiag\,}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, and for
\theta=1
, we
have

\omega(1,t,s)=(1+\mathrm{i})(t+s),\quad\widetilde{\omega}(1,t,s)=(1+\mathrm{i})(t-s)\,,

so
\Gamma_{1}=(1+\mathrm{i})\mathbb{R}\times(1+\mathrm{i})\mathbb{R}
is a
Cartesian product.

In Proposition
3.5
and the subsequent identities we
establish a Stokes’ formula for the transformation of (
3.14
)
and get as in (
3
)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\pi}

\displaystyle\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathrm{adiag\,}({\mathbb{C}}^{2})}\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\Gamma_{1-\delta}}\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{1-\delta}\frac{2\mathrm{i}\,\overline{\widetilde{\zeta}}}{1-\theta^{2}}\,a(\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta};h)\chi(\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta})}\,\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{1-\delta}\hskip-5.0pt\frac{1}{\pi}\hskip-5.0pt\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\Gamma_{\theta}}\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\widetilde{\nu}(\theta;\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\rfloor\mathrm{d}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\big(\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\big)\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=:\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1-\delta)+\mathrm{II}(\zeta,1-\delta)+\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1-\delta)\,.

Here
\widetilde{\nu}
is a deformation vector field, as explained in
Proposition
3.5
. In the applications we will be able to pass to
the limit
\delta=0
, see (
3
).

We now assume that
\phi
is holomorphic in

\mathrm{neigh}((0,0);\mathbb{C}^{2})
(either in
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\xi,\eta}
or in
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}
, depending on the
which coordinates we use). Assume (
4.23
)

\phi(0,0)=0,\ \ \nabla\phi(0,0)=0\,.

Let
\phi_{2}(\xi,\eta)
be the 2nd order Taylor polynomial of
\phi
at
(0,0)
, then as in (
4.24
)

\phi(\xi,\eta)=\phi_{2}(\xi,\eta)+{\mathcal{O}}(|(\xi,\eta)|^{3})\,.

We use the same notations for functions in the variables
\omega,\widetilde{\omega}
,

\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})=\phi_{2}(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})+{\mathcal{O}}(|(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})|^{3})\,.

Assume that
\phi_{2}
is real-valued on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\xi,\eta}
,
hence as in (
4.26
)

\phi_{2}(\xi,\eta)=\frac{\lambda}{2}\xi^{2}-\frac{\mu}{2}\eta^{2}+\rho\,\xi\eta,\ \ \lambda,\mu,\rho\in\mathbb{R}\,.

Assume (
4.27
):

\lambda,\,\mu>0.

We assume (
4.56
):

a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})={\mathcal{O}}(1)\hbox{ is holomorphic in }\mathrm{neigh}\big((0,0);\mathbb{C}^{2}\big)\,.

We allow
a
to depend on
h
and then assume that
a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)
is a classical symbol of order

h^{0}
, holomorphic in
(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})
, in the sense that

a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\sim\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}a_{j}(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})h^{j}\hbox{ in }\,C^{\infty}\Big(\mathrm{neigh}\big((0,0);\mathbb{C}^{2}\big);\mathbb{C}\Big)\,,

(1.12)

where
a,a_{j}
are holomorphic in the same
\mathrm{neigh}\big((0,0);\mathbb{C}^{2}\big)
.
We will call such a symbol a
holomorphic classical symbol.

From now on, we will work with the class of holomorphic classical symbols. Note that this framework lies between the frameworks of classical symbols and analytic symbols. This framework is sufficient in this paper, since our goal is to study the asymptotic behaviour of the integral (
1.1
), using the complex contour deformation method, but it suffices to allow error terms of order
\mathcal{O}(h^{\infty})
. Here we do not need a certain stability as in the framework of pseudo-differential operators with analytic symbols, for example for the composition or invertibility of these operators; in particular, there is no need to introduce a family of pseudo-norms. For more details on the framework of classical symbol spaces and analytic symbol spaces, see
[
6
]
and
[
19
]
, see also
[
3
,
2
,
18
,
17
]
.

We assume that
\chi
is supported in the neighborhood in
(
4.56
) and that (
4.57
) holds,

\chi=1\hbox{ in }\mathrm{neigh}\big((0,0);\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\big)\,.

Assume (
1.11
):

|\zeta|\leq h^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}\hbox{ for some fixed }\delta\in]0,1/6[\,.

Assume that

\begin{split}&\zeta\neq 0\hbox{ and that }\zeta\,\hbox{ is not in some fixed conic}\\
&\hbox{neighborhood of }\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\left({\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}\right)\hbox{ in }{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}\,.\end{split}

(1.13)

Here we notice that if
\zeta\neq 0
belongs to a small conic
neighborhood of
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
in
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}
, then we can make a small rotation:
\xi+\mathrm{i}\eta=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}(\check{\xi}+i\check{\eta})
(equivalently
\omega=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}\check{\omega}
,
\widetilde{\omega}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\theta}\check{\widetilde{\omega}}
).
Replace
\omega
with
\check{\omega}
in (
1.2
) and notice that

\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\theta}\frac{1}{\check{\omega}-\check{\zeta}}\,,\quad\check{\zeta}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\theta}\zeta\,,

so
\check{\zeta}
avoids a small conic neighborhood of
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}{\mathbb{R}}
in
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}
. The slightly rotated new
exponent
\phi(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\theta}\check{\omega})
still satisfies
(
4.26
).

Proposition 1.1
.

Under the above assumptions the integrals
\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)
,
\mathrm{II}(\zeta,1)
,
\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1)
are well defined and respectively equal to

\underset{\delta\to 0}{\lim}\,\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1-\delta)
,
\underset{\delta\to 0}{\lim}\,\mathrm{II}(\zeta,1-\delta)
,
\underset{\delta\to 0}{\lim}\,\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1-\delta)
, so by (
3
),

I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)=\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)+\mathrm{II}(\zeta,1)+\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1)\,.

To describe the asymptotics of
I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)
it
suffices to do so for
\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)
,
\mathrm{II}(\zeta,1)
,
\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1)
.

1.

The term
\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)
is studied in Section
4
. Since

\Gamma_{1}
is a Cartesian product, we can study the first integral
with respect to
\widetilde{\omega}
and apply stationary phase. This
leads to (
4.65
)

\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\zeta-\omega}h^{\frac{1}{2}}b(\omega;h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\psi(\omega)}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,,

where up to an exponentially small term
b
is an analytic symbol
truncated to a neighborhood of
\omega=0
and
\psi(\omega)
is
holomorphic near
0
with Taylor expansion

\psi(\omega)=\psi_{2}(\omega)+{\mathcal{O}}(\omega^{3})\,,\quad\psi_{2}(\omega)=f\,\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}\,,

where
f
is given by (
4.83
):

f=\frac{\lambda\mu+\rho^{2}}{\lambda+\mu+2\mathrm{i}\rho}\,.

In and around (
4.93
)–(
4.102
) we discuss the entire
special functions related to Dawson’s integral. In the result below, these
functions are used only in regions where they are not exponentially
large. Our main result for the term
\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)
is given in
Proposition
4.4
:

Assume that
|\zeta|\leq h^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}
and that
(
1.13
) holds. Define
\widehat{\zeta}
and
\zeta^{\dagger}
with
|\widehat{\zeta}|,\,|\zeta^{\dagger}|\leq{\mathcal{O}}(h^{-\delta})
, by
\widehat{\zeta}:=h^{-1/2}\zeta=:(\mathrm{i}/f)^{1/2}\zeta^{\dagger}
. Then there exists an asymptotics series

c(\widehat{\omega};h)\sim c_{0}(\widehat{\omega})+hc_{1}(\widehat{\omega})+...
, where
c_{j}(\widehat{\omega})
is a
polynomial of degree
\leq 3j
(independent of
\zeta
) such that
(
4.109
) holds,

\begin{split}\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)&=h^{\frac{1}{2}}c(f^{-1}D_{\widehat{\zeta}};h)\left(G_{\mathrm{i}\psi_{2}}(\widehat{\zeta})\right)+{\mathcal{O}}(h^{\infty})\\
&=h^{1/2}c((\mathrm{i}/f)^{1/2}\partial_{\zeta^{\dagger}};h)\big(G^{l/r}_{-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}}(\zeta^{\dagger})\big)+{\mathcal{O}}(h^{\infty})\,.\end{split}

The functions
G_{\rho}=G_{\rho}^{l/r}
are defined by line integrals in
(
4.88
), (
4.94
) and we take
G_{..}=G_{..}^{l}
when
\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\widehat{\zeta})>0
and

G_{..}=G_{..}^{r}
when
\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\widehat{\zeta})<0
.

Assume that
|\zeta|\leq h^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}
. Then, by (
4.60
), (
4.43
), the leading term of the
\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)
is given by:

h^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\frac{2\sqrt{2\pi}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}}{(\lambda+\mu+\mathrm{i}2\rho)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,a(0,0)\,G_{-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}}\Big(h^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{f}\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\zeta\Big)\,,

(1.14)

where

G_{-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}}(\zeta)=\begin{cases}G_{-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}}^{l}(\zeta)\quad\mathrm{when}\quad\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\zeta)>0\\
G_{-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}}^{r}(\zeta)\quad\mathrm{when}\quad\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}{\zeta})<0\end{cases}\,.

2.

We next discuss
\mathrm{II}(\zeta,1)
.

Proposition
5.2
gives a representation of
\mathrm{II}(\zeta,1)
:

Let
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\mathbb{R}
. Then

s_{+}=\mathrm{Re}(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}{\zeta}/{|\zeta|})\neq 0
, so
\widetilde{\zeta}(t)\to\infty
when
t\to+\infty

and
\mathrm{II}(\zeta,1)
is well defined and as in (
5.12
) it
is equal to

\int_{0}^{+\infty}b(\zeta,t)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{h}\varphi(\zeta,t)}\,{\mathrm{d}t}\,,

where

b(\zeta,t)=a(\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta}(t);h)\,\chi(\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta}(t))\,\dfrac{\mathrm{i}\overline{\widetilde{\zeta}(t)}}{\sqrt{1+t^{2}}}\,,

\varphi(\zeta,t)=\mathrm{i}\phi(\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta}(\zeta,t)),

and

\widetilde{\zeta}(t):=\widetilde{\zeta}(\zeta,t)
are given by (
5.7
), (
5.8
) and (
5.9
) respectively.
For
0\neq\zeta=|\zeta|\sigma
,
\sigma\in S^{1}
,

\widetilde{\zeta}(t)=|\zeta|\left(2s_{+}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}t+\overline{\sigma}g_{1}(t)\right),

where

\sigma=s_{+}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}+s_{-}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}
with

s_{\pm}=\mathrm{Re}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\sigma\right),
satisfying

s_{+}^{2}+s_{-}^{2}=1
,
and

g_{1}(t):=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1+t^{2}}+t}\,,\ \ t\geq 0\,,

satisfies

\partial_{t}^{k}g_{1}(t)={\mathcal{O}}\left((1+t)^{-1-k}\right),

for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

We also have as in (
5.73
)

|\partial_{t}\varphi(\zeta,t)|\asymp|\zeta|^{2}t,\hbox{ when }t\in[T_{0},+\infty[\hbox{ and }(\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta}(\zeta,t))\in\mathrm{supp}(\chi),

when
T_{0}>0
is large enough, allowing us to define as in (
5.37
),

c^{(N)}=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\Big(-\frac{h}{|\zeta|^{2}\partial_{t}\varphi_{2}}\partial_{t}\Big)^{k}\frac{h}{|\zeta|^{2}\partial_{t}\varphi_{2}}b=:\sum_{k=0}^{N}c_{k}\,.

Here
\varphi_{2}(\zeta,t):=\mathrm{i}\phi_{2}(\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta}(\zeta,t))
, see (
5.18
).

Recalling that
\varepsilon\leq h^{-\delta}
we split this region into
two and start with the case of
intermediate values of
\varepsilon

(cf. (
5.29
)), i.e.

h^{\delta}\leq\varepsilon\,\leq h^{-\delta}\,.

In this case we have Proposition
5.7
:

Assume that
\chi
in (
4.58
) has
sufficiently small support.
For
T\geq T_{0}
,
T\gg\frac{\sqrt{h}}{|\zeta|}=\varepsilon^{-1}
, we have for every
b\in|\zeta|\mathbb{S}^{0}([0,+\infty[)
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
:

\int_{T}^{+\infty}b(t)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{|\zeta|^{2}}{h}\varphi(\sigma,t)}\,{\mathrm{d}t}=-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{|\zeta|^{2}}{h}\varphi(\sigma,T)}\left(c^{(N)}(T)+\mathcal{O}(1)\left(\frac{h}{(T|\zeta|)^{2}}\right)^{N+1}\frac{h}{T|\zeta|}\right).

Here
c^{(N)}
is defined as in (
5.37
), but with
\varphi_{2}

replaced by
\varphi
, (
5.43
) remains valid. Here
\mathbb{S}^{0}([0,+\infty[)
is a symbol space given by (
5.24
), see definition
5.4
.

The integral
\int_{0}^{T}b(t)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{|\zeta|^{2}}{h}\varphi(\sigma,t)}\,{\mathrm{d}t}
can be computed with standard numerical
techniques for the values of
\varepsilon
and
T
considered here.

We next turn to the
the small
\varepsilon
limit:

\varepsilon\leq h^{\delta}\,.

We can make asymptotics expansions in
\varepsilon
and get as in
(
5.143
), (
5.144
), with
M\geq 2L
,

\frac{1}{h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{+\infty}b(\zeta,t;h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{h}\varphi(\zeta,t)}\,{\mathrm{d}t}\\
={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\left(h^{\frac{N}{2}-1}+\varepsilon^{2L}\right)+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)+\hbox{a linear
combination of terms}\\
\sum_{j=0}^{K}h^{\frac{j}{2}+\frac{\widetilde{K}}{2}-n}\varepsilon^{2\nu}\left(\sum_{\mu=0}^{j+\widetilde{K}}\varepsilon^{j+\widetilde{K}-\mu}\frac{\widetilde{d}_{\mu}}{\varepsilon}+\sum_{\mu=-M-1}^{-1}\varepsilon^{j+\widetilde{K}}\widetilde{d}_{\mu}\varepsilon^{-\mu-1}\ln(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\right)

with

K,\widetilde{K},N,\nu\in\mathbb{N},\ \nu<L,\ \frac{\widetilde{K}}{2}-\left[\frac{\widetilde{K}}{3}\right]<\frac{N}{2}-1,\ n\leq\left[\frac{\widetilde{K}}{3}\right]\,.

Here
\widetilde{d}_{\mu}
also depends on
j,\widetilde{K},\nu
and

\sigma
.
We also have (
5.2
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}b(\zeta,t;h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{h}\varphi(\zeta,t)}\,{\mathrm{d}t}={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\left(h^{\frac{N}{2}-1}+\varepsilon^{2L}\right)+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)+\hbox{a linear
}

\displaystyle\hbox{ combination of terms }\varepsilon^{2\nu}h^{\frac{\widetilde{K}}{2}-n}\sum_{j=0}^{K}h^{\frac{j}{2}}\left(p_{j}(\varepsilon)\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{j+\widetilde{K}}q_{j}(\varepsilon)\ln(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\right),

where
p_{j}
,
q_{j}
are polynomials in
\varepsilon

still with (
5.144
) valid.
Here
\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)
is at most
\mathcal{O}(1)

with respect to the two parameters
h
and
\varepsilon
.

The leading term in
\mathrm{II}(\zeta,1)
is given by
(
5.149
):

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}&b(\zeta,t;h)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{h}\varphi(\zeta,t)}\mathrm{d}t\\
&=h^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{s_{+}(\sigma)}{|s_{+}(\sigma)|}\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}\,a(0,0)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}}{(\lambda+\mu+\mathrm{i}2\rho)^{\frac{1}{2}}}+{\mathcal{O}}(h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon)+{\mathcal{O}}(h)+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{2})\,,\end{split}

where
s_{+}(\sigma):=\mathrm{Re}(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\sigma)
with
\sigma=\frac{\zeta}{|\zeta|}\in S^{1}
.

3.

As for
\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1)
,
we show in Section

6
that

\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1)={\mathcal{O}}(h^{\infty})\,.

The main result of this paper is the asymptotics behaviour of the integral (
1.1
) (same as (
2.4
),
(
3.14
)) in the regime
|\zeta|\in]0,h^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}[
with
\delta>0
. Recalling (
1.14
), (
5.149
) and (
6.23
), we obtain

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
V\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
be an open neighborhood of
0
,

\phi\in C^{\infty}(V;{\mathbb{R}})
. Assume (
2.1
),
(
2.2
), (
2.3
). For
|\zeta|\in]0,h^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta}[
with
\delta\in]0,\frac{1}{6}[
satisfying (
1.13
), define

I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)
as in (
2.4
) with
a
is a holomorphic classical symbol, and
\chi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(V)
equal
to 1 near
0
. Then, the leading term
\big(
modulo
\mathcal{O}(h^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta})\big)
of
I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)
is given by:

h^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\frac{2\sqrt{2\pi}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}a(0,0)}{(\lambda+\mu+\mathrm{i}2\rho)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,\,\times\begin{cases}G_{-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}}^{l}\Big(h^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{f}\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\zeta\Big)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\quad\mathrm{when}\quad\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\zeta)>0\\
{}\quad{}\,{}\\
G_{-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}}^{r}\Big(h^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{f}\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\zeta\Big)-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\quad\mathrm{when}\quad\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}{\zeta})<0\,,\end{cases}

where
f
is given by (
4.83
). Moreover, by (
B.9
), the leading term
\big(
modulo
\mathcal{O}(h^{\frac{1}{2}+2\delta})\big)
of
I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)
becomes

h^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\frac{2\sqrt{\pi}\,a(0,0)}{(\lambda+\mu+\mathrm{i}2\rho)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,\mathrm{sgn}\Big(\mathrm{Re}(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\zeta)\Big)\,,

(1.15)

where
\mathrm{sgn}(x)=\frac{x}{|x|}\,,x\in\dot{\mathbb{R}}
.

Remark 1.3
.

Note that if we further assume that
|\zeta|\geq\exp(-\exp(1/{\mathcal{O}}(h))
, then we prove that the three terms

\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)
,
\mathrm{II}(\zeta,1)
and
\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1)
obtained via Stokes’ formula are invariant under rotations modulo an exponentially small error. As a result, the dominant term of the integral (
1.1
) is then independent of the choice of parameterization of the deformation contours.

The paper is organized as follows
: In Section 2 we give a direct
approach to deal with the case where the pole is far from the
critical point of the phase. In
Section 3 we propose the decomposition of the integral (
1.1
)
via Stokes’ formula. The first term
\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)
of this decomposition is discussed
in Section 4, the second in Section 5, and we prove that the third
term is
\mathcal{O}(h^{\infty}),
in Section 6.
We add some concluding remarks in Section 7.
In Appendix A, we briefly review some elements of the theory of wavefront sets.
We present in Appendix B additional information on the special function appearing in the asymptotics expression of the first term
\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)
of the decomposition of the integral (
1.1
) via Stokes’ formula. Appendix C is devoted to the invariance of
\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)
under rotations.

2
Direct approach when
\frac{|\zeta|}{\sqrt{h}}\gg 1

In this section we discuss the case that the
singularity is well separated from the stationary
point,
|\zeta|\gg\sqrt{h}
, where a standard steepest descent
approach can be
applied.

Let
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{C}
be an open neighborhood of
0
, let
\phi\in C^{\infty}(V;\mathbb{R})
satisfy

\phi(0)=0,\qquad\phi^{\prime}(0)=0\,,

(2.1)

{\rm det}\big(\phi^{\prime\prime}(0)\big)\not=0\,,

(2.2)

\phi^{\prime}(\omega)\not=0,\hbox{ when }\omega\in V\setminus\{0\}\,.

(2.3)

We are interested in the asymptotics behaviour of

I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega;h)\chi(\omega)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega)}\,L(\mathrm{d}\omega)\,,

(2.4)

where
a(\omega;h)\in C^{\infty}(V\times]0,h_{0}])
is a classical symbol of order
h^{0}
, i.e. such that

a(\omega;h)\sim a_{0}(\omega)+ha_{1}(\omega)+\ldots\hbox{ in
}C^{\infty}(V),\quad h\to 0\,,

(2.5)

and
\chi(\omega)\in C_{0}^{\infty}(V)
is a fixed cutoff function, equal
to 1 near
\omega=0
.

The main contribution to the integral comes from a small neighborhood
of
\{0,\zeta\}\in\mathbb{C}_{\omega}
, where
0
is the critical
point of the exponent and
\zeta
is the locus of the pole
singularity of the integrand.

In this section we study the easy case when

\varepsilon:=\frac{|\zeta|}{\sqrt{h}}\gg 1\,.

(2.6)

In this case it is possible to separate the contributions from
\omega=0
and from
\omega=\zeta
. We start with the even easier case when

|\zeta|\geq\frac{1}{\mathcal{O}(1)}\,.

(2.7)

Let
\chi_{0}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(V)
have support in a small neighborhood of

\omega=0
and be equal to 1 in another such neighborhood of 0. Then
\chi=\chi_{0}+\chi_{1}
, where
\chi_{1}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(V)
,
0\not\in\mathrm{supp}(\chi_{1})
,
\zeta\not\in\mathrm{supp}(\chi_{0})
. Write

I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)=I(a\chi_{0},\phi,\zeta;h)+I(a\chi_{1},\phi,\zeta;h)\,.

(2.8)

The function

\omega\longmapsto\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega;h)\chi_{0}(\omega)

vanishes for
\omega
close to
\zeta
and is therefore a classical
symbol of order
h^{0}
. By stationary phase we get (cf.
[
6
, Chapter
5]
)

I(a\chi_{0},\phi,\zeta;h)=b(\zeta;h)\,,

(2.9)

where

b\sim h\big(b_{1}+hb_{2}+\ldots\big)

(2.10)

is a classical symbol of order
h
with

b_{1}(\zeta)=\frac{2}{\zeta}\,a_{0}(0)\,\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}\mathrm{sgn}\phi^{\prime\prime}(0)}}{|\mathrm{det}\phi^{\prime\prime}(0)|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,.

(2.11)

Near
\mathrm{supp}(\chi_{1})
, we have
\phi^{\prime}(\omega)\not=0
and it is
straight forward to construct a classical symbol
c(\omega;h)
of
order
h
such that

a(\omega;h)\chi_{1}(\omega)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega)}=\partial_{\overline{\omega}}\Big(c(\omega;h)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega)}\Big)+\mathcal{O}(h^{\infty})\,,

(2.12)

where
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{\infty})
denotes a remainder term which is
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{N})
uniformly in
\omega
, for every
N>0
.
Here

c\sim h\big(c_{1}(\omega)+hc_{2}(\omega)+\ldots\big)

(2.13)

with

c_{1}(\omega)=\frac{a_{0}(\omega)\chi_{1}(\omega)}{\mathrm{i}\partial_{\overline{\omega}}\phi(\omega)}\,.

(2.14)

Moreover
\mathrm{supp}\big(c(\cdot;h)\big)\subset\mathrm{supp}(\chi)
.

Since formally
I(a\chi_{1},\phi,\zeta;h)=\partial_{\overline{\zeta}}^{-1}\left(a\chi_{1}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi}\right)
, we expect that

I(a\chi_{1},\phi,\zeta;h)=c(\zeta;h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\zeta)}+\mathcal{O}(h^{\infty})\,.

(2.15)

One can prove this by substituting (
2.12
) in the expression for
I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)
(see (
2.4
)), integrate by parts:

I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)=\mathcal{O}(h^{\infty})+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\partial_{\overline{\omega}}\Big(\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\Big)c(\omega;h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega)}\,L(\mathrm{d}\omega)

and use that

\partial_{\overline{\omega}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\right)=\pi\delta_{\zeta}(\omega)\,,

where
\delta_{\zeta}(\omega)
is the Dirac measure at
\zeta
. This gives (
2.15
).

Combining (
2.15
), (
2.9
) we get when

|\zeta|\geq\frac{1}{\mathcal{O}(1)}

I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)=c(\zeta;h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\zeta)}+b(\zeta;h)+\mathcal{O}(h^{\infty})\,,

(2.16)

where the symbols
b,c
are of order
h
and satisfy (
2.14
), (
2.11
).

We now allow
|\zeta|
to be small and assume that

|\zeta|\ll 1,\qquad\frac{|\zeta|}{\sqrt{h}}=:\varepsilon\gg 1\,.

(2.17)

We shall use a cutoff

\widetilde{\chi}_{0}\left(\omega/|\zeta|\right)\,,

(2.18)

where
\widetilde{\chi}_{0}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is equal to 1 on the disc

D(0,2)
3
3

3

For
r>0,

D(0,r)=\{x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\,x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}<r\}
.
, so that

\widetilde{\chi}_{0}\left(\omega/|\zeta|\right)=1
in the disc

D(\zeta,2|\zeta|)
. Consider

J:=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\big(1-\widetilde{\chi}_{0}(\omega/|\zeta|)\big)}{\omega-\zeta}\,\,\,a(\omega;h)\chi(\omega)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega)}\,L(\mathrm{d}\omega)

(2.19)

and make repeated integration by parts, using that

|\phi^{\prime}(\omega)|\asymp|\omega|\,,\qquad\frac{h}{\mathrm{i}}\frac{1}{|\phi^{\prime}(\omega)|^{2}}\phi^{\prime}(\omega)\cdot\nabla_{\omega}\big(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega)}\big)=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega)}\,.

(2.20)

We write
X\asymp Y
for
X,Y\in\mathbb{R}
if
X,Y
have the same sign (or
vanish) and
X=\mathcal{O}(Y)
and
Y=\mathcal{O}(X)
. Here we review
\omega\cong(\mathrm{Re}(\omega),\mathrm{Im}(\omega))
as an element of
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\mathrm{Re}(\omega),\mathrm{Im}(\omega)}.

The adjoint of

\frac{1}{|\phi^{\prime}(\omega)|^{2}}\phi^{\prime}(\omega)\cdot\nabla_{\omega}
is equal
to

L:=\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{2}\partial_{\omega_{j}}\circ\Big(\frac{\partial_{\omega_{j}}\phi(\omega)}{|\phi(\omega)|^{2}}\Big)\,,

where
w=\xi+\mathrm{i}\eta\cong(\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We get

J=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega)}\Big(\frac{h}{\mathrm{i}}L\Big)^{N}\Big(\frac{\big(1-\widetilde{\chi}_{0}(\omega/|\zeta|)\big)}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega;h)\chi(\omega)\Big)\,L(\mathrm{d}\omega)\,.

Here
|\zeta-\omega|\geq\frac{|\omega|}{2}
on

\mathrm{supp}\big(1-\widetilde{\chi}_{0}(\omega/|\zeta|)\big)
and we see that

J=\mathcal{O}(1)\int_{2|\zeta|\leq|\omega|\leq\frac{1}{\mathcal{O}(1)}}\,\,\frac{h^{N}}{|\omega|^{2N+1}}\,\,L(\mathrm{d}\omega)\,.

Here
\displaystyle\frac{h}{|\omega|^{2}}\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\frac{h}{|\zeta|^{2}}=\mathcal{O}(1)\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}
for
\omega
in the domain of integration so

J=\mathcal{O}(1)\varepsilon^{-2N}\int_{2|\zeta|\leq|\omega|\leq\frac{1}{\mathcal{O}(1)}}\,\frac{1}{|\omega|}\,L(d\omega)=\mathcal{O}(1)\varepsilon^{-2N}\,.

(2.21)

We conclude that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)=I(a\widetilde{\chi}_{0}(\cdot/|\zeta|),\phi,\zeta;h)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-2N})\,.

(2.22)

In order to study

(a\widetilde{\chi}_{0}(\cdot/|\zeta|),\phi,\zeta;h)
, we put

\zeta=\lambda\widetilde{\zeta}
, where
\lambda\asymp|\zeta|
is an
extra parameter. Make the change of variables

\omega=\lambda\widetilde{\omega}
. Write

\frac{1}{h}\phi(\omega)=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{h}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi(\lambda\widetilde{\omega})=\frac{1}{\widetilde{h}}\widetilde{\phi}(\widetilde{\omega})\,,

(2.23)

where

\widetilde{h}=\frac{h}{\lambda^{2}}\asymp\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\ll 1,\quad\widetilde{\phi}(\widetilde{\omega})=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi(\lambda\widetilde{\omega})\,,

(2.24)

so the remainder in (
2.22
) is
\mathcal{O}\big(\,{\widetilde{h}}^{N}\big)
.

Clearly
\widetilde{\phi}(0)=0
,
\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime}(0)=0
,
\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime\prime}(0)=\phi^{\prime\prime}(0)
and we get

I(a\widetilde{\chi}_{0}(\cdot/|\zeta|),\phi,\zeta;h)=-\frac{\lambda}{\pi}\int\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}-\widetilde{\zeta}}\,\widetilde{a}(\widetilde{\omega})\widetilde{\chi}_{0}((\lambda/|\zeta|)\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\tilde{h}}\tilde{\phi}(\widetilde{\omega})}L(\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega})\,,

(2.25)

where

\widetilde{a}(\widetilde{\omega};\widetilde{h})=a(\omega;h)=a(\lambda\widetilde{\omega};\lambda^{2}\widetilde{h})\,.

(2.26)

Since
\lambda\leq 1
,
\widetilde{h}\geq h
,
\widetilde{a}
is a holomorphic
classical symbol with
\widetilde{h}
as the new semiclassical
parameter, uniformly with respect to
\lambda
. Moreover

|\widetilde{\zeta}|\asymp 1
so
\widetilde{\zeta}
is
well separated from
\widetilde{\omega}=0
. We can therefore apply the
asymptotic formula (
2.16
) to

I(a\widetilde{\chi}_{0,\lambda},\phi,\zeta;h)
in (
2.25
) and
get for
I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)
in (
2.22
):

I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)=\mathcal{O}\big(\widetilde{h}^{\infty}\big)+\lambda\Big(\widetilde{c}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})\,\mathrm{e}^{{\mathrm{i}}\tilde{\phi}(\widetilde{\omega})/\widetilde{h}}+\widetilde{b}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})+\mathcal{O}\big(\widetilde{h}^{\infty}\big)\Big)\,,

(2.27)

where
\widetilde{c}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h}),\widetilde{b}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})
are classical symbols of order
\widetilde{h}
satisfying the analogues of (
2.13
), (
2.10
):

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\widetilde{c}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})\sim\widetilde{h}\big(\widetilde{c}_{1}(\widetilde{\zeta})+\widetilde{h}\widetilde{c}_{2}(\widetilde{\zeta})+\ldots\,\big)\\
\\
\widetilde{b}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})\sim\widetilde{h}\big(\widetilde{b}_{1}(\widetilde{\zeta})+\widetilde{h}\widetilde{b}_{2}(\widetilde{\zeta})+\ldots\,\big)\end{array}\right.

(2.28)

with

\widetilde{b}_{1}(\widetilde{\zeta})=\frac{2}{\widetilde{\zeta}}\,\,\widetilde{a}_{0}(0)\,\,\dfrac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}\mathrm{sgn}\phi^{\prime\prime}(0)}}{|\mathrm{det}\phi^{\prime\prime}(0)|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,,

(2.29)

\widetilde{c}_{1}(\widetilde{\zeta})=\dfrac{\widetilde{a}_{0}(\widetilde{\zeta})}{\mathrm{i}\partial_{\overline{\tilde{\zeta}}}\tilde{\phi}(\tilde{\zeta})}\,.

(2.30)

On the formal asymptotics level we have

\lambda\,\widetilde{c}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})=c(\zeta;h)\,,\qquad\lambda\,\widetilde{b}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})=b(\zeta;h)\,.

(2.31)

If
\lambda\geq\dfrac{1}{\mathcal{O}(1)}h^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}
,
or equivalently

\widetilde{h}\leq\mathcal{O}(1)h^{\frac{\delta}{2}}\,,

(2.32)

for some
\delta>0
, then the remainders
\mathcal{O}\big((\widetilde{h})^{\infty}\big)
and
\lambda\mathcal{O}\big((\widetilde{h})^{\infty}\big)
are
\mathcal{O}(h^{\infty})
.

The following proposition summarizes the results of this section

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
V\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
be an open neighborhood of
0
,

\phi\in C^{\infty}(V;{\mathbb{R}})
. Assume (
2.1
),
(
2.2
), (
2.3
). For
\zeta={\mathcal{O}}(1)
, define

I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)
as in (
2.4
) with the classical
symbol
a
as in (
2.5
) and
\chi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(V)
equal
to 1 near
0
.

In any open set where
|\zeta|\asymp 1
, we have
(
2.16
), where
c
,
b
are classical symbols of order
h
,
satisfying (
2.10
), (
2.11
), (
2.13
),
(
2.14
).

Assume now (
2.6
), let the parameter
\lambda
be

\asymp|\zeta|
and put

\widetilde{h}=h/\lambda^{2}\asymp\varepsilon^{-2}
, so that

\widetilde{h}\ll 1
. Define the rescaled variable

\widetilde{\omega}
by
\omega=\lambda\widetilde{\omega}
and
the rescaled functions
\widetilde{\phi}(\widetilde{\omega})
,

\widetilde{a}(\widetilde{\omega};\widetilde{h})
by (
2.24
),
(
2.26
). Put
\zeta=\lambda\widetilde{\zeta}
. Then we have (
2.27
), where

\widetilde{c}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h}),\widetilde{b}(\widetilde{\zeta};\widetilde{h})
are classical symbols of order

\widetilde{h}
, satisfying (
2.28
), (
2.29
),
(
2.30
). If

\widetilde{h}\leq\mathcal{O}(1)h^{\frac{\delta}{2}}
, for some

\delta>0
, then the remainders

\mathcal{O}\big(\widetilde{h}^{\infty}\big)
and

\lambda\mathcal{O}\big(\widetilde{h}^{\infty}\big)
are

\mathcal{O}(h^{\infty})
. (
2.27
) coincides with (
2.16
)
in the sense of formal asymptotics expansions and is therefore
independent of the choice of
\lambda\asymp|\zeta|
.

3
Decomposition by Stokes’ formula

In this section, we apply Stokes’ formula to the
integral on steepest descent contours in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. This leads
to a decomposition of the studied integral into three integrals.

We recall a contour deformation version of Stokes’ formula (cf.

[
19
, lemme 12.2]
). Let
0<k<n
be integers. Let
f:\,I\times W\ni(t,x)\mapsto f(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a smooth map, where

I\Subset\mathbb{R}
,
W\Subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
are open and connected. We
introduce the integration contour

W\ni x\mapsto f(t,x)=:f_{t}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(3.1)

When
f_{t}
is injective with injective differential we can view

\Gamma_{t}:=f_{t}(W)
as a submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, at least locally.

Let
\Omega
be a smooth differential
k
-form on some open
subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\pi_{x}(f^{-1}(\mathrm{supp\,}\Omega))\Subset W\,,

(3.2)

where
\pi_{x}:I\times W\to W
is the natural projection. Let

\nu_{t}=\partial_{t}f(t,x)\in T_{f_{t}(x)}\mathbb{R}^{n}\,.

(3.3)

Lemma 3.1
.

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{t}}\hskip-3.0pt\Omega
is a smooth function of
t
and we have

\partial_{t}\int_{\Gamma_{t}}\hskip-3.0pt\Omega=\int_{\Gamma_{t}}\hskip-3.0pt\nu_{t}\rfloor\mathrm{d}\Omega\,.

(3.4)

Here
\nu_{t}\rfloor\mathrm{d}\Omega
denotes the contraction of
\mathrm{d}\Omega
with
\nu_{t}
.

Proof.

With the differential form
\widetilde{\Omega}=f^{*}\Omega
on

I\times W
and

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{t}=\{(t,x);\,x\in W\}

(
3.4
) amounts to

\partial_{t}\int_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{t}}\widetilde{\Omega}=\int_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{t}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\rfloor\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\Omega}\,,

(3.5)

which follows from a simple calculation.
\square

Remark 3.2
.

Assume that
f
is injective with injective differential, so that

f
is a diffeomorphism (at least locally):
I\times W\to\Gamma
,
where
\Gamma
is a smooth submanifold of dimension
k+1
. Then there is a
smooth vector field
\nu
on
\Gamma
, such that
\nu(x)=\nu_{t}(x)

when
x\in\Gamma_{t}
and
\Gamma_{t}
are
k
-dimensional smooth
submanifolds of
\Gamma
. Clearly,

\Gamma_{s}=\big(\exp(s-t)\nu\big)\big(\Gamma_{t}\big)\,,\quad s,t\in I\,.

(3.6)

It follows that

\int_{\Gamma_{s}}\Omega=\int_{\Gamma_{t}}(\exp(s-t)\nu)^{*}\Omega\,,

(3.7)

and taking the derivative with respect to
s
at
s=t
,

\partial_{t}\int_{\Gamma_{t}}\Omega=\int_{\Gamma_{t}}{\mathcal{L}}_{\nu}(\Omega)\,,

(3.8)

where
{\mathcal{L}}_{\nu}
denotes Lie derivative with respect to
\nu
. Using Cartan’s identity, we recover (
3.4
):

\int_{\Gamma_{t}}{\mathcal{L}}_{\nu}(\Omega)=\int_{\Gamma_{t}}\nu\rfloor\mathrm{d}\Omega+\int_{\Gamma_{t}}\mathrm{d}(\nu\rfloor\Omega)\,.

(3.9)

Here
{{\nu\rfloor\Omega}_{|}}_{\Gamma_{t}}
has compact support
so the integral of its differential, i.e. the last term in
(
3.9
), vanishes.

Remark 3.3
.

We make the same geometric assumptions as in Remark
3.2
and
relax the smoothness assumption on
\Omega
: Assume now that
\Omega
is a
k
-form with distribution coefficients satisfying
(
3.2
) and

\mathrm{WF}(\Omega)\cap N^{*}(\Gamma_{t})=\emptyset,\ t\in I\,,

(3.10)

where
N^{*}(\Gamma_{t})
denotes the conormal bundle of
\Gamma_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then
{{\Omega}_{|}}_{\Gamma_{t}}
is a well
defined
k
form on
\Gamma_{t}
with distribution coefficients and

I\ni t\mapsto{{\Omega}_{|}}_{\Gamma_{t}}

is smooth with values in
the distributional
k
-forms in the sense that
I\ni t\mapsto\int_{\Gamma_{t}}\phi\Omega
is smooth for all
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and (
3.4
) holds. See Appendix
A
for more
details and a review of wavefront sets.

Let
\phi(\xi,\eta)
be analytic near
(0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

and let
a(\xi,\eta;h)
be a holomorphic classical symbol defined
near the same point. Let
\chi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be equal
to 1 near
(0,0)
and with support contained in the domains of
definition of
\phi
and
a
. We will continue the study of the integral

I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{(\xi+\mathrm{i}\eta)-\zeta}\,a(\xi,\eta;h)\chi(\xi,\eta)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\xi,\eta)}\,\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\eta

(3.11)

for
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
.
For this we will use the method of polarization and start by
identifying
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\xi,\eta}
with the antidiagonal

\mathrm{adiag\,}(\mathbb{C}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}^{2})=\{(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}};\,\,\,\widetilde{\omega}=\overline{\omega}\}\,.

(3.12)

Here we put

\omega=\xi+\mathrm{i}\eta,\hbox{ and also }\widetilde{\omega}=\xi-\mathrm{i}\eta\,.

(3.13)

Since

\mathrm{d}\xi\wedge\mathrm{d}\eta=\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}},

the integral in (
3.11
) can be written

I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathrm{adiag\,}({\mathbb{C}}^{2})}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\,,

(3.14)

abusing slightly the notation, letting
a
,
\chi
,
\phi
denote
“the same” functions on
\mathrm{adiag\,}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
using
(
3.13
) and the inverse relations

\xi=\frac{\omega+\widetilde{\omega}}{2},\quad\eta=\frac{\omega-\widetilde{\omega}}{2\mathrm{i}}\,.

(3.15)

Later we will assume that
\phi(\xi,\eta)
is real valued on the
real domain with
(0,0)
as a non-degenerate critical point and such
that this is the only critical point of
\phi
on the real domain.
One would like to apply the method of stationary phase, but the
singularity at
\omega=\zeta
poses an obstacle for that, especially
when
|\zeta|\leq{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{h})
. (When
|\zeta|\gg\sqrt{h}
,
we separate the contributions from
the critical point at
\omega=0
and from the singularity at
\omega=\zeta
. See Section
2
.)

The idea is then to deform the contour
\mathrm{adiag}({\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}})
to a new contour in
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}
which is a Cartesian product

\gamma_{1}\times\gamma_{2}
, where
\gamma_{1}\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega}

and
\gamma_{2}\subset{\mathbb{C}}_{\tilde{\omega}}
are
curves. Stokes’ formula will then produce several terms to be
analyzed, in particular the integral along
\gamma_{1}\times\gamma_{2}
which
reduces to 1D integrals:

\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\gamma_{1}\times\gamma_{2}}(...)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega=\int_{\gamma_{1}}\left(\int_{\gamma_{2}}(...)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\right)\mathrm{d}\omega\,.

(3.16)

We will apply Lemma
3.1
with

\Omega=-\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\,,

(3.17)

where
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{0}\big(\mathbb{C}^{2};[0,1]\big)
is a
smooth cutoff function equal to
1
near (0,0) with support inside the
domains where
a
,
\phi
are holomorphic.

We define a smooth family of contours
\Gamma_{\theta}
,
\theta\in[0,1]
, of real dimension 2 in
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\xi,\eta}
or equivalently in
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}
. In the
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\xi,\eta}
representation it is
given by

\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(t,s)\mapsto(\xi(\theta,t,s),\eta(\theta,t,s))\in\mathbb{C}^{2},

where

\begin{cases}\xi(\theta;t,s)=t+\mathrm{i}\theta t=(1+\mathrm{i}\theta)t\\
\eta(\theta;t,s)=s-\mathrm{i}\theta s=(1-\mathrm{i}\theta)s\\
\end{cases},\quad(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,.

(3.18)

In the
{\mathbb{C}}_{\omega,\widetilde{\omega}}
representation it is
given by

\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(t,s)\,\mapsto f(\theta;t,s)=\big(\omega(\theta;t,s)\,,\,\widetilde{\omega}(\theta;t,s)\big)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}},

(3.19)

where

\begin{cases}\omega(\theta;t,s):&\hskip-9.0pt=\xi(\theta;t,s)+\mathrm{i}\eta(\theta;t,s)\\
&\hskip-9.0pt=(1+\mathrm{i}\theta)t+\mathrm{i}(1-\mathrm{i}\theta)s\\
&\hskip-9.0pt=(t+\mathrm{i}s)+\mathrm{i}\theta(t-\mathrm{i}s)\\
\widetilde{\omega}(\theta;t,s):&\hskip-9.0pt=\xi(\theta;t,s)-\mathrm{i}\eta(\theta;t,s)\\
&\hskip-9.0pt=(1+\mathrm{i}\theta)t-\mathrm{i}(1-\mathrm{i}\theta)s\\
&\hskip-9.0pt=(t-\mathrm{i}s)+\mathrm{i}\theta(t+\mathrm{i}s)\\
\end{cases}\,,\quad(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

(3.20)

hence

f(\theta;t,s)=\big((1+\mathrm{i}\theta)t+\mathrm{i}(1-\mathrm{i}\theta)s,(1+\mathrm{i}\theta)t-\mathrm{i}(1-\mathrm{i}\theta)s\big)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\,.

(3.21)

In the
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\xi,\eta}
representation, we have
\Gamma_{\theta}=(1+\mathrm{i}\theta)\mathbb{R}\times(1-\mathrm{i}\theta)\mathbb{R}
, especially
\Gamma_{0}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
In the
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}
representation, we
get
\Gamma_{0}=\mathrm{adiag\,}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, and for
\theta=1
, we
have

\begin{cases}\omega(1,t,s)&=(1+\mathrm{i})(t+s)=\sqrt{2}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}(t+s)\\
\widetilde{\omega}(1,t,s)&=(1+\mathrm{i})(t-s)=\sqrt{2}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}(t-s)\end{cases}\,,

so
\Gamma_{1}=(1+\mathrm{i})\mathbb{R}\times(1+\mathrm{i})\mathbb{R}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\mathbb{R}
is a
Cartesian product.

Identifying
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}
with the
underlying real space
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\mathrm{Re}\,\omega,\mathrm{Im}\,\omega,\mathrm{Re}\,\tilde{\omega},\mathrm{Im}\,\tilde{\omega}}
, we see that

\mathrm{WF\,}(\Omega)
is contained in the conormal bundle of the
set
\{(\omega,\widetilde{\omega});\,\,\,\omega=\zeta\}
viewed as
a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. We can therefore define the restriction

{{\Omega}_{|}}_{\Gamma_{\theta}}
for a given fixed
\theta\in[0,1]
if the conormal bundle of
\Gamma_{\theta}
has zero
intersection with the conormal bundle of
\{(\omega,\widetilde{\omega});\,\,\,\omega=\zeta\}
. Since our manifolds are affine spaces of the
same dimension 2, this is equivalent to the property that they intersect at a single point or still that

\Gamma_{\theta}\ni(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\longmapsto\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}\hbox{ is injective}\,.

In view of (
3.20
) this is equivalent to the implication

\begin{cases}t+\mathrm{i}s+\mathrm{i}\theta(t-\mathrm{i}s)=0\\
t,s\in\mathbb{R}\end{cases}\Longrightarrow\,\,\,\,t=s=0\,,

which, by separating real and imaginary parts, amounts to

\det\begin{pmatrix}1&\theta\\
\theta&1\end{pmatrix}\neq 0\,,

i.e.
\theta^{2}\neq 1
. In conclusion,

{{\Omega}_{|}}_{\Gamma_{\theta}}\hbox{ is well defined when }0\leq\theta<1.

(3.22)

The deformation vector field in (
3.3
),

\nu(\theta,\cdot):=\partial_{\theta}f\big(\theta,\omega(\theta;\cdot),\widetilde{\omega}(\theta,\cdot)\big)
, is given
along the contour
\Gamma_{\theta}
by

\begin{split}\nu(\theta)&=\big(\partial_{\theta}\omega\big)\cdot\partial_{\omega}+\big(\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{\omega}\big)\cdot\partial_{\widetilde{\omega}}+\big(\partial_{\theta}\overline{\omega}\big)\cdot\partial_{\overline{\omega}}+\big(\partial_{\theta}\overline{\widetilde{\omega}}\big)\cdot\partial_{\overline{\tilde{\omega}}}\\
&=:\nu_{\omega}(\theta)\cdot\partial_{\omega}+\nu_{\tilde{\omega}}(\theta)\cdot\partial_{\tilde{\omega}}+\nu_{\overline{\omega}}(\theta)\cdot\partial_{\overline{\omega}}+\nu_{\overline{\tilde{\omega}}}(\theta)\cdot\partial_{\overline{\tilde{\omega}}}\,,\end{split}

(3.23)

and more explicitly by

\nu(\theta,\cdot)=\mathrm{i}(t-\mathrm{i}s)\cdot\partial_{\omega}+\mathrm{i}(t+\mathrm{i}s)\cdot\partial_{\tilde{\omega}}-\mathrm{i}(t+\mathrm{i}s)\cdot\partial_{\overline{\omega}}-\mathrm{i}(t-\mathrm{i}s)\cdot\partial_{\overline{\tilde{\omega}}}\,.

(3.24)

Now, fix
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
and apply Lemma
3.1
to obtain
for
0\leq\theta<1
,

\frac{\mathrm{d}I}{\mathrm{d}\theta}=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\Gamma_{\theta}}\nu(\theta;\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\right),

(3.25)

where
I(\theta):=I(\Gamma_{\theta};\zeta)
is given by

\hskip-7.0ptI(\theta):=I(\Gamma_{\theta};\zeta)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\Gamma_{\theta}}\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{w};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\,.

(3.26)

Further, in the sense of differential forms with coefficients that
are distributions on
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}
with
singular support contained in
\{(\omega,\widetilde{\omega});\,\,\,\omega=\zeta\}
,

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}&\left(\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\right)\\
=&\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{\omega}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\\
&+\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{\widetilde{\omega}}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\right)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\widetilde{\omega}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\,,\\
=&\pi\delta_{\zeta}(\omega)\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\\
&+\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\frac{\partial\chi}{\partial\overline{\omega}}(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\\
&+\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{w};h)\,\frac{\partial\chi}{\partial\overline{\widetilde{\omega}}}(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\widetilde{\omega}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\,.\end{split}

(3.27)

Here we use that
a
and
\phi
are holomorphic.

Equality (
3.27
) can be written more briefly as

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi\delta_{\zeta}(\omega)\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}

(3.28)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\,\mathrm{d}_{\omega,\widetilde{\omega}}\Big(\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\Big)\,.

Pointwise in
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}
we have that

\partial_{\omega},\,\partial_{\overline{\omega}},\,\partial_{\tilde{\omega}},\,\partial_{\overline{\tilde{\omega}}}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\mathrm{d}\omega,\,\mathrm{d}{\overline{\omega}},\,\mathrm{d}{\widetilde{\omega}},\,\mathrm{d}{\overline{\widetilde{\omega}}}

are dual bases in

\mathbb{C}\otimes T\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\hbox{ and }\mathbb{C}\otimes T^{*}\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\hbox{ respectively.}

This gives

\begin{split}&{\partial_{\omega}}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\omega=1,\quad{\partial_{\omega}}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}=0,\quad\partial_{\omega}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}=0,\quad\partial_{\omega}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\overline{\widetilde{\omega}}=0\,,\\
&\partial_{\overline{\omega}}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\omega=0,\quad{\partial_{\overline{\omega}}}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}=1,\quad\partial_{\overline{\omega}}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}=0,\quad\partial_{\overline{\omega}}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\overline{\widetilde{\omega}}=0\,,\\
&\partial_{\tilde{\omega}}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\omega=0,\quad\partial_{\tilde{\omega}}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\overline{{\omega}}=0,\quad{\partial_{\tilde{\omega}}}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}=1,\quad{\partial_{\tilde{\omega}}}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\overline{\widetilde{\omega}}=0\,,\quad\\
&{\partial_{\overline{\tilde{\omega}}}}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\omega=0,\quad{\partial_{\overline{\tilde{\omega}}}}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}=0,\quad{\partial_{\overline{\tilde{\omega}}}}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}=0,\quad{\partial_{\overline{\tilde{\omega}}}}\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\overline{\widetilde{\omega}}=1\,.\end{split}

(3.29)

Then by (
3
), (
3.24
) and (
3.29
), we obtain

\displaystyle\nu(\theta;\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}_{\omega,\widetilde{\omega}}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\right)

(3.30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}(t-\mathrm{i}s)\pi\delta_{\zeta}(\omega)a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\,\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}}{2\mathrm{i}}

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}(t+\mathrm{i}s)\pi\delta_{\zeta}(\omega)a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\,\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}(t+\mathrm{i}s)\pi\delta_{\zeta}(\omega)a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\,\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}

\displaystyle+0

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\nu(\theta;\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\Big(\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\Big)\,.

Here
(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is given in terms of
(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})
as follows. Recall (
3.20
) and take complex conjugates:

\begin{cases}\omega=(t+\mathrm{i}s)+\mathrm{i}\theta(t-\mathrm{i}s)\\
\overline{\omega}=-\mathrm{i}\theta(t+\mathrm{i}s)+(t-\mathrm{i}s)\end{cases}\,.

(3.31)

Then

\begin{cases}t+\mathrm{i}s=\dfrac{1}{1-\theta^{2}}\big(\omega-\mathrm{i}\theta\overline{\omega}\big)\\
t-\mathrm{i}s=\dfrac{1}{1-\theta^{2}}\big(\mathrm{i}\theta\omega+\overline{\omega}\big)\end{cases}\,.

(3.32)

Combining (
3.20
), (
3.32
) gives

\begin{cases}\widetilde{\omega}=\dfrac{1}{1-\theta^{2}}\big(2\mathrm{i}\theta\omega+(1+\theta^{2})\overline{\omega}\big)\\
\overline{\widetilde{\omega}}=\dfrac{1}{1-\theta^{2}}\big((1+\theta^{2}){\omega}-2\mathrm{i}\theta\overline{\omega}\big)\end{cases}\,.

(3.33)

Equivalently,

\begin{cases}\overline{\omega}=\dfrac{-2\mathrm{i}\theta}{1+\theta^{2}}\omega+\dfrac{1-\theta^{2}}{1+\theta^{2}}\widetilde{\omega}\\
{}\\
\overline{\widetilde{\omega}}=\dfrac{1-\theta^{2}}{1+\theta^{2}}{\omega}-\dfrac{2\mathrm{i}\theta}{1+\theta^{2}}\widetilde{\omega}\end{cases}\,.

(3.34)

By (
3.32
), the expression (
3.30
) becomes

\begin{split}\nu(\theta;\omega,&\widetilde{\omega})\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\right)\\
=&\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{1-\theta^{2}}\big(\mathrm{i}\theta\omega+\overline{\omega}\big)\pi\delta_{\zeta}(\omega)\,a(\omega,\widetilde{w};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\,\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}}{2\mathrm{i}}\\
&-\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{1-\theta^{2}}\big(\omega-\mathrm{i}\theta\overline{\omega}\big)\pi\delta_{\zeta}(\omega)a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\,\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\\
&-\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{1-\theta^{2}}\big(\omega-\mathrm{i}\theta\overline{\omega}\big)\pi\delta_{\zeta}(\omega)a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\,\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\\
&+\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\nu(\theta;\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\Big(\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\Big)\,,\end{split}

(3.35)

or equivalently

\displaystyle\hskip-6.0pt\nu(\theta;\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\right)=S_{l}+S_{r}

(3.36)

with

\displaystyle S_{l}:=\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{1-\theta^{2}}

\displaystyle\pi\delta_{\zeta}(\omega)a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\times

(3.37)

\displaystyle\left[\big(\mathrm{i}\theta\omega+\overline{\omega}\big)\,\,\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}}{2\mathrm{i}}-\big(\omega-\mathrm{i}\theta\overline{\omega}\big)\,\,\,\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}+\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\Big)\right]

and

S_{r}:=\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\nu(\theta;\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\Big(\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\Big)\,.

(3.38)

In order to study
\displaystyle\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\Gamma_{\theta}}\hskip-4.0ptS_{\ell}
we shall express the
restrictions to
\Gamma_{\theta}
of
\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}
,
\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega
,
\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega
as
multiples of
L(\mathrm{d}\omega)
.
Identities (
3.33
) and (
3.20
) give

\displaystyle L(\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}):=\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\widetilde{\omega}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}}{2\mathrm{i}}=-L(\mathrm{d}\omega)=-(1-\theta^{2})\,\mathrm{d}t\wedge\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}{\omega}}{2\mathrm{i}}=\frac{1+\theta^{2}}{1-\theta^{2}}L(\mathrm{d}\omega)=(1+\theta^{2})\,\mathrm{d}t\wedge\mathrm{d}s

(3.39)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}}{2\mathrm{i}}=\frac{2\mathrm{i}\theta}{1-\theta^{2}}L(\mathrm{d}\omega)=2\mathrm{i}\theta\,\mathrm{d}t\wedge\mathrm{d}s\,.

Then (
3.37
) becomes

\hskip-9.0ptS_{l}=\dfrac{-2\mathrm{i}\left[(1+\theta^{2})\omega-2\mathrm{i}\theta\overline{\omega}\right]}{(1-\theta^{2})^{2}}\Big(\pi\delta_{\zeta}(\omega)a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\Big)\,\,L(\mathrm{d}\omega)\,.

(3.40)

Recalling (
3.33
), we get

S_{l}=\dfrac{-2\mathrm{i}\,\overline{\widetilde{\omega}}}{1-\theta^{2}}\Big(\pi\delta_{\zeta}(\omega)\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\Big)\,\,L(\mathrm{d}\omega)\,.

(3.41)

This gives

-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\Gamma_{\theta}}S_{l}=\dfrac{2\mathrm{i}\,\overline{\widetilde{\zeta}}}{1-\theta^{2}}\,a(\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta};h)\,\chi(\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta})}\,,

(3.42)

where
\widetilde{\zeta}
is determined by the condition that
(\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta})\in\Gamma_{\theta}
, i.e. by (
3.33
) with
\omega,\widetilde{\omega}
replaced by
\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta}
:

\widetilde{\zeta}=\dfrac{1}{1-\theta^{2}}\,\,\Big(2\mathrm{i}\theta\zeta+(1+\theta^{2})\overline{\zeta}\Big)\,,

(3.43)

i.e.

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\Gamma_{\theta}}\hskip-1.0ptS_{l}=

\displaystyle\frac{2\mathrm{i}\Big((1+\theta^{2}){\zeta}-2\mathrm{i}\theta\overline{\zeta}\Big)}{(1-\theta^{2})^{2}}\,\,\,\,a\Big(\zeta,\frac{1}{1-\theta^{2}}\,\big(2\mathrm{i}\theta\zeta+(1+\theta^{2})\overline{\zeta}\big);h\Big)\,\times

(3.44)

\displaystyle\chi\Big(\zeta,\frac{1}{1-\theta^{2}}\,\big(2\mathrm{i}\theta\zeta+(1+\theta^{2})\overline{\zeta}\big)\Big)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi\big(\zeta,\frac{1}{1-\theta^{2}}\,\,(2\mathrm{i}\theta\zeta+(1+\theta^{2})\overline{\zeta}\big)}\,.

We next show that the restriction of
\nu\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\Big(\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega\Big)
to
\Gamma_{\theta}

for
0\leq\theta\leq 1
, is independent of the choice of smooth deformation field
\nu
for the family
\Gamma_{\theta}
: Indeed let
\widetilde{\nu}
be a second deformation field for the same family. Then
\mu:=\widetilde{\nu}-\nu
is a real vector field which is tangent to
\Gamma_{\theta}
for any fixed
\theta\in[0,1]
and it suffices to show that

\left(\mu\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\Big(\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega\Big)\right)_{\big|\displaystyle\Gamma_{\theta}}=0\,.

(3.45)

Proof
of (
3.45
). Since
\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega_{\big|\displaystyle\Gamma_{\theta}}
is a 2-form and hence of maximal degree, we have

\mathrm{d}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\Big(\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega\Big)_{\big|\displaystyle\Gamma_{\theta}}=0
and since
\mu:=\nu-\widetilde{\nu}
is tangential to
\Gamma_{\theta}
we have

\begin{split}\left(\mu\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\Big(\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega\Big)\right)_{\big|\displaystyle\Gamma_{\theta}}&=\mu\mathord{\rfloor}\left(\mathrm{d}\big(\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega\big)\right)_{\big|\displaystyle\Gamma_{\theta}}\\
&=\mu\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}\Big(\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega_{\big|\displaystyle\Gamma_{\theta}}\Big)\\
&=0\,.\end{split}

(3.46)

Here we used that if
\mu
is a vector field tangent to
\Gamma_{\theta}
and
\Omega
is a
k
-form, then

\mu\rfloor\left({{\Omega}_{|}}_{\Gamma_{\theta}}\right)={{(\mu\rfloor\Omega)}_{|}}_{\Gamma_{\theta}}.

Indeed, this is clear when
\Omega
is a 1-form and in the general
case we get it when
\Omega
is an exterior product of 1-forms
and
more generally when
\Omega
is a linear combination of such exterior
products.
\square

(3.47)

4
The term
\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1-\delta)
in (
3
)

In this section the leading order contributions to
the first term in the decomposition of Section
3
are
identified.

We shall work in coordinates that are well adapted to
\Gamma_{\theta}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\theta\in[0,1]
. Then

\begin{split}&e_{+}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\frac{1-\theta}{\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\,,\,\frac{1+\theta}{\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\right)\in\Gamma_{\theta}\,,\\
&e_{-}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\frac{1+\theta}{\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\,,\,\frac{1-\theta}{\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\right)\in\Gamma_{\theta}\end{split}

(4.1)

form an orthonormal basis in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
,
hence also an orthonormal basis in
\Gamma_{\theta}
as a Euclidean
space.

Proof.

By (
3.20
),
\Gamma_{\theta}
is given by

\begin{cases}\omega=\tau+\mathrm{i}\theta\overline{\tau}\\
\widetilde{\omega}=\overline{\tau}+\mathrm{i}\theta\tau\end{cases}\,,\,\tau=t+\mathrm{i}s\,\,\,\,\mathrm{with}\,\,\ t,s\in\mathbb{R}.

Removing a common positive prefactor, put

\begin{split}\widetilde{e}_{+}&=((1-\theta)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}},(1+\theta)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}})=:(\omega_{+},\widetilde{\omega}_{+})\,,\\
\widetilde{e}_{-}&=((1+\theta)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}},(1-\theta)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}})=:(\omega_{-},\widetilde{\omega}_{-})\,.\end{split}

Then

\begin{cases}\omega_{+}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}-\theta\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}+\mathrm{i}\theta\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}=\tau+\mathrm{i}\theta\overline{\tau}\\
\widetilde{\omega}_{+}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}+\theta\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}+\mathrm{i}\theta\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}=\overline{\tau}+\mathrm{i}\theta\tau\end{cases}\,,\qquad\tau=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\,,

\begin{cases}\omega_{-}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}+\theta\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}+\mathrm{i}\theta\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}=\tau+\mathrm{i}\theta\overline{\tau}\\
\widetilde{\omega}_{-}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}-\theta\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}+\mathrm{i}\theta\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}=\overline{\tau}+\mathrm{i}\theta\tau\end{cases}\,,\quad\tau=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\,,

so
\widetilde{e}_{\pm}
and hence also
e_{\pm}
belong to
\Gamma_{\theta}
. By direct calculation, we see that
e_{+}
,
e_{-}
form an
orthonormal basis in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
and the lemma follows.

\square

Every
(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\in\Gamma_{\theta}

has a unique decomposition

(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})=w_{+}e_{+}+w_{-}e_{-}\,,

(4.2)

where
w_{\pm}=((\omega,\widetilde{\omega})|e_{\pm})_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}
are
real (and this still holds for
(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
now with
w_{\pm}\in\mathbb{C}
).
We have more direct formulae for
w_{\pm}
by looking at the

\omega
-component of (
4.1
), (
4.2
),

\omega=w_{+}\frac{1-\theta}{\sqrt{2(1+\theta^{2})}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}+w_{-}\frac{1+\theta}{\sqrt{2(1+\theta^{2})}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}

(4.3)

and using that
(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}},\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}})
is an orthonormal basis in
\mathbb{C}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2}
, leading to

w_{+}=\frac{\sqrt{2(1+\theta^{2})}}{1-\theta}\mathrm{Re}(\omega\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}),\quad w_{-}=\frac{\sqrt{2(1+\theta^{2})}}{1+\theta}\mathrm{Re}(\omega\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}})\,.

(4.4)

Similarly, by taking the
\widetilde{\omega}
components in
(
4.1
), (
4.2
),

\widetilde{\omega}=w_{+}\frac{1+\theta}{\sqrt{2(1+\theta^{2})}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}+w_{-}\frac{1-\theta}{\sqrt{2(1+\theta^{2})}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\,,

(4.5)
