. By scaling

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}},\qquad\varepsilon>0.

(5.119)

Differentiate with respect to
\varepsilon
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\varepsilon\,t^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}t^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{2}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\,,\quad c_{2}=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\not=0\,.

(5.120)

Repeating the argument gives

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}t^{2k}\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{c_{2k}}{\varepsilon^{2k+1}}\,,\quad c_{2k}:=\frac{(2k)!}{2^{k}k!}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\not=0\,.

(5.121)

Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}([0,+\infty[;[0,1])
,
\chi(t)=1
for
t\gg 1
and
\chi(t)=0
for
0\leq t\ll 1
. Then

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\chi(t)t^{2k}\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{c_{2k}}{\varepsilon^{2k+1}}+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)\,,

(5.122)

where
\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)
denotes a function, analytic near
\varepsilon=0
.

In order to reach
k\leq 0
, we take
k=0
in (
5.122
):

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{c_{0}}{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)\,,\quad c_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\not=0\,,

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\varepsilon\,t^{2}\frac{1}{t^{2}}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t={c_{0}}+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon).

Take primitives with respect to
\varepsilon
:

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\frac{1}{t^{2}}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=c_{-2}-{c_{0}}\varepsilon+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)=\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)

with
c_{-2}=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{\chi(t)}{t^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t<+\infty
. Recall that
\chi^{(p)}(0)=0
for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
.

Iterate:

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\frac{1}{t^{2k}}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon),\qquad k=1,2,\ldots\,.

(5.123)

Next, start from

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2}}t\,\mathrm{d}t=1

(5.124)

and scale

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}t\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\,.

(5.125)

Differentiate with respect to
\varepsilon
:

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}t^{3}\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{c_{3}}{\varepsilon^{4}}\quad\hbox{ with }\,c_{3}=2

and more generally,

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}t^{2k+1}\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{c_{2k+1}}{\varepsilon^{2k+2}}\quad\hbox{ with }\,c_{2k+1}=2^{k}k!\,,\,\,\hbox{ for all }\,k\in\mathbb{N}\,.

(5.126)

To go in the opposite direction, start from

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\chi(t)t\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)\,.

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\varepsilon\,t^{2}\frac{1}{t}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)

and integrate with respect to
\varepsilon
:

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\frac{1}{t}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\ln\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)\,.

Write this as

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\varepsilon\,t^{2}\frac{1}{t^{3}}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\varepsilon\ln\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)

and integrate with respect to
\varepsilon

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\frac{1}{t^{3}}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=c_{-3}\varepsilon^{2}\ln\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)\,.

Iterate:

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\frac{1}{t^{k}}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=c_{-k}\varepsilon^{k-1}\ln\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)\,,

(5.127)

for
1\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
odd. Summing up, we have for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\hskip-10.0pt\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\,{t^{k}}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\begin{cases}c_{k}\varepsilon^{-1-k}+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon),&\hbox{ if }k\geq 0\,,\\
\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon),&\hbox{ if }0\not=k\in-2\mathbb{N},\\
c_{k}\varepsilon^{-1-k}\ln(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)\,,&\hbox{ if }k\in-(2\mathbb{N}+1)\,.\\
\end{cases}

(5.128)

Using (
5.128
), we compute for any fixed
\tau\in\mathbb{C}
the following integral

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\,(t+\tau)^{k}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,.

(5.129)

In fact, we get

—

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\begin{split}\hskip-20.0pt\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\,(t+\tau)^{k}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t&=\sum_{j=0}^{k}d_{j}^{(k)}\varepsilon^{-1-j}+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)\\
&=\frac{d_{k}^{(k)}}{\varepsilon^{k+1}}+\frac{d_{k-1}^{(k)}}{\varepsilon^{k}}+\cdots+\frac{d_{0}^{(k)}}{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)\,,\end{split}

(5.130)

where for all
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}\,,\quad\displaystyle d_{j}^{(k)}=\binom{k}{j}\tau^{j}\times\begin{cases}\frac{(2p)!}{2^{p}p!}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}&\mathrm{if}\,\,\,j=2p\\
{}&\\
2^{p}p!&\mathrm{if}\,\,\,j=2p+1\end{cases}
.

—

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}\,(t+\tau)^{k}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\underset{-k+l\,\mathrm{is\,odd}}{\sum_{l=0}^{N}}d_{l}^{(k)}

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-1-k+l}\ln(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})+

(5.131)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-k+N-\delta})+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)\,,

for any fixed integer
N
and a positive real number
\delta
small enough (
0<\delta\ll 1
).
Note that the coefficients
d_{l}^{(k)}
’s are computable.

(
5.130
) and (
5.131
) remain valid with modified coefficients,
if we replace
\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}
by
\mathrm{e}^{-\widetilde{\varepsilon}^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}
where
\widetilde{\varepsilon}
is a constant with
\mathrm{Re}(\widetilde{\varepsilon})>0
.

Notice that if
f=\mathcal{O}\Big((t+1)^{-N-2}\Big)
, then

F(\varepsilon):=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}t^{2}}\,f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\in C^{N}\big(\mathrm{neigh}(0);\mathbb{R}\big)

(5.132)

and by Taylor expansion

F(\varepsilon)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{N-1}(\partial_{\varepsilon}^{\nu}F)(0)\,\,\frac{\varepsilon^{\nu}}{\nu!}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N})\,.

(5.133)

We return to (
5.117
) and write the general term on the RHS as

\sum_{j=0}^{N}h^{\frac{j}{2}+\frac{\widetilde{K}}{2}-n}\varepsilon^{2\nu}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon^{2}\varphi^{0}_{2}(\sigma,t)}\,\widetilde{\upsilon}(\sigma,t)^{\nu}\varepsilon^{j+\widetilde{K}}b_{j}(t)\widetilde{r}_{\widetilde{K}}(\sigma,t)\,dt

(5.134)

with
\widetilde{K},N,\nu,\widetilde{\upsilon},b_{j},\widetilde{r}_{\widetilde{K}}
as
after (
5.117
) and in (
5.118
).

We split the integral in two by means of
1=(1-\chi)+\chi
with
\chi

as above.
(1-\chi)
is supported in a bounded interval
[0,R[
and we see that

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon^{2}\varphi^{0}_{2}(\sigma,t)}\,\widetilde{\upsilon}(\sigma,t)^{\nu}\varepsilon^{2\nu+j+\widetilde{K}}b_{j}(t)\widetilde{r}_{\widetilde{K}}(\sigma,t)(1-\chi(t))\,\mathrm{d}t=\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)\,.

(5.135)

Recall that
\varphi^{0}_{2}(\sigma,t)
is given by (
5.87
) with
\upsilon=0
. The results (
5.130
) (
5.131
) remain valid for

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon^{2}\varphi^{0}_{2}(\sigma,t)}t^{k}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t.

(5.136)

In fact, using a contour deformation to reach the critical point

\tau_{c}:=-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\frac{\lambda-\mu}{2s_{+}(\lambda+\mu+\mathrm{i}2\rho)}\sigma

of
t\mapsto\varphi^{0}_{2}(\sigma,t)
and the fact that the function
\chi
is equal to 1 for large
t
, we show that modulo an analytic function of
\varepsilon
the integral in (
5.136
) is equal to that given by (
5.129
) with
\tau=\tau_{c}
, modified as mentioned after (
5.131
).

We get the same symbol space
\mathbb{S}^{m}_{cl}([T,+\infty[)
for large
T,
if we replace all powers of
t
by powers of
\widetilde{t}:=2s_{+}t+\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\dfrac{\lambda-\mu}{\lambda+\mu+\mathrm{i}2\rho}\,\sigma
and it easy to go from one representation to the other.

Next look at the complementary term in the splitting of the integral
in (
5.134
), namely
h^{\frac{j}{2}+\frac{\widetilde{K}}{2}-n}
times

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon^{2}\varphi^{0}_{2}(\sigma,t)}\,\widetilde{\upsilon}(\sigma,t)^{\nu}\varepsilon^{2\nu+j+\widetilde{K}}b_{j}(t)\widetilde{r}_{\widetilde{K}}(\sigma,t)\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,.

(5.137)

In view of the above remark we may shift to the variable
\widetilde{t}
, where
\varphi^{0}_{2}(\sigma,t)=-C\varepsilon^{2}\widetilde{t}^{2}
,
\mathrm{Re}(C)>0
,
then drop the tildes and reduce us (essentially) to the case

\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon^{2}\varphi^{0}_{2}(\sigma,t)}=\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}t^{2}}
.

We have
\widetilde{\upsilon}^{\nu}b_{j}\widetilde{r}_{\widetilde{K}}\in\mathbb{S}_{cl}^{j+\widetilde{K}}([T,+\infty[)
,
\mathrm{supp}\chi\subset[T,+\infty[
and for
M\in\mathbb{N}
,
M\gg 1
,

\widetilde{\upsilon}^{\nu}b_{j}\widetilde{r}_{\widetilde{K}}(t)=\sum_{\mu=-M-1}^{j+\widetilde{K}}d_{\mu}t^{\mu}+\mathcal{O}\Big((t+1)^{-M-2}\Big)\,.

(5.138)

Insert the different terms of RHS(
5.138
) into (
5.137
):

—

When
0\leq\mu\leq j+\widetilde{K}
, we get in view of (
5.130
)

\varepsilon^{2\nu+j+\widetilde{K}-\mu}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{2}^{0}(\sigma,t)}\varepsilon^{\mu}d_{\mu}t^{\mu}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\varepsilon^{2\nu+j+\widetilde{K}-\mu}\,\Big(\frac{\widetilde{d}_{\mu}}{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)\Big)\,.

(5.139)

—

When
\mu\leq-1
, we get

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2\nu+j+\widetilde{K}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{2}^{0}(\sigma,t)}d_{\mu}t^{\mu}\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\varepsilon^{2\nu+j+\widetilde{K}}\Big(\widetilde{d}_{\mu}\varepsilon^{-\mu-1}\ln(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})

(5.140)

\displaystyle+\underset{-\mu+l\,\mathrm{is\,odd}}{\sum_{l=0}^{N}}\widetilde{d}_{l}^{(\mu)}\varepsilon^{-1-\mu+l}\ln(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-\mu+N-\delta})+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)\Big)\,.

Finally from (
5.132
), (
5.133
), we get

\varepsilon^{2\nu+j+\widetilde{K}}\hskip-5.0pt\int_{0}^{+\infty}\hskip-5.0pt\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{2}^{0}(\sigma,t)}\mathcal{O}\left((t+1)^{-M-2}\right)\chi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\varepsilon^{2\nu+j+\widetilde{K}}\left(\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{M})\right)\,.

(5.141)

Combining (
5.137
)-(
5.141
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon^{2}\varphi^{0}_{2}(\sigma,t)}\widetilde{\upsilon}(\sigma,t)^{\nu}\varepsilon^{2\nu+j+\widetilde{K}}b_{j}(t)\widetilde{r}_{\widetilde{K}}(\sigma,t)\chi(t)\mathrm{d}t

(5.142)

\displaystyle=\hskip-10.0pt\sum_{0\leq\mu\leq j+\widetilde{K}}\hskip-10.0pt\varepsilon^{2\nu+j+\widetilde{K}-\mu}\frac{\widetilde{d}_{\mu}}{\varepsilon}+\hskip-10.0pt\sum_{-M-1\leq\mu\leq-1}\hskip-10.0pt\varepsilon^{2\nu+j+\widetilde{K}}\widetilde{d}_{\mu}\varepsilon^{-\mu-1}\ln(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon^{2\nu+j+\widetilde{K}}\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{M})+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)\,.

Use this and (
5.135
) in (
5.117
) with
M\geq 2L
,

\frac{1}{h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{+\infty}b(\zeta,t;h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{h}\varphi(\zeta,t)}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\left(h^{\frac{N}{2}-1}+\varepsilon^{2L}\right)+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)+\hbox{a linear
combination of terms}\\
\sum_{j=0}^{N}h^{\frac{j}{2}+\frac{\widetilde{K}}{2}-n}\varepsilon^{2\nu}\left(\sum_{\mu=0}^{j+\widetilde{K}}\varepsilon^{j+\widetilde{K}-\mu}\frac{\widetilde{d}_{\mu}}{\varepsilon}+\sum_{\mu=-M-1}^{-1}\varepsilon^{j+\widetilde{K}}\widetilde{d}_{\mu}\varepsilon^{-\mu-1}\ln(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\right)

(5.143)

with

\widetilde{K},N,\nu\in\mathbb{N},\ \nu<L,\ \frac{\widetilde{K}}{2}-\left[\widetilde{K}/3\right]<\frac{N}{2}-1,\ n\leq\left[\widetilde{K}/3\right]\,.

(5.144)

Here
\widetilde{d}_{\mu}
also depends on
j,\widetilde{K},\nu
and

\sigma
. The sum in RHS(
5.143
) can be written

\varepsilon^{2\nu}\sum_{j=0}^{N}\left(h^{\frac{j}{2}+\frac{\widetilde{K}}{2}-n}p_{j}(\varepsilon)\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{j+\widetilde{K}}h^{\frac{j}{2}+\frac{\widetilde{K}}{2}-n}q_{j}(\varepsilon)\ln(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\right)\,,

where
p_{j},q_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,N
are polynomials in
\varepsilon
and
after extracting some powers of
h
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon\,}\int_{0}^{+\infty}b(\zeta,t;h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{h}\varphi(\zeta,t)}\,\mathrm{d}t={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\left(h^{\frac{N}{2}-1}+\varepsilon^{2L}\right)+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)+\hbox{a linear
}

\displaystyle\hbox{ combination of terms }\varepsilon^{2\nu}h^{\frac{\widetilde{K}}{2}-n}\sum_{j=0}^{N}h^{\frac{j}{2}}\left(p_{j}(\varepsilon)\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{j+\widetilde{K}}q_{j}(\varepsilon)\ln(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\right)

(5.145)

still with (
5.144
) valid.

The leading term in (
5.107
)
.

We insert (
5.113
) into the general terms in RHS(
5.107
)
and use (
5.77
) where

\frac{\overline{\widetilde{\zeta}}(\zeta,t)}{\sqrt{1+t^{2}}}=h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon\frac{2s_{+}(\sigma)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}t+\sigma g_{1}(t)}{\sqrt{1+t^{2}}}=h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon\Big(2s_{+}(\sigma)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}+{\mathcal{O}}((1+t)^{-2})\Big).

The leading contribution to (
5.107
) is then

\int_{0}^{+\infty}h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon 2s_{+}(\sigma)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{2}^{0}(\sigma,t)}a(0,0)\,\mathrm{d}t={\mathcal{O}}(h^{\frac{1}{2}})\,,

(5.146)

obtained by taking the leading term
a(0,0)
in (
5.113
),
\nu=0
and the trivial product
``\overset{0}{\underset{j=1}{\prod}}(...)"=1
. For all other
terms we gain some power of
\varepsilon
or
h^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Notice that the
remainder
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})(h^{N/2}+\varepsilon\,^{2L})
is to be
replaced by
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{\frac{1}{2}})(h^{N/2}+\varepsilon\,^{2L})
since our

b
is now
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon\,)
rather than
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
.

In conclusion
, for
b
given in
(
5.77
), we have after comparing the asymptotic decomposition
(
5.113
), (
5.115
), (
5.117
),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}b(\zeta,t;h)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{h}\varphi(\zeta,t)}\,\mathrm{d}t=h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon

\displaystyle 2s_{+}(\sigma)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}a(0,0)\times

(5.147)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{2}^{0}(\sigma,t)}\,\mathrm{d}t+{\mathcal{O}}(h)+{\mathcal{O}}(h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon)+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{2})\,,

where the first term on the right hand side is
{\mathcal{O}}(h^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

Recall that
\varphi_{2}^{0}(\zeta,t)
is given by RHS(
5.87
)
with "
\upsilon
" there replaced by
0
.
Remembering the treatment of (
5.136
) with
k=0
we see that

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{2}^{0}(\sigma,t)}\,\mathrm{d}t&=\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)+\int_{0}^{+\infty}\exp\left(-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{\lambda+\mu+\mathrm{i}2\rho}{8}(2s_{+}t)^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)+\int_{0}^{+\infty}\exp\left(-\varepsilon^{2}s_{+}^{2}(\lambda+\mu+\mathrm{i}2\rho)\frac{t^{2}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\mathrm{an(\varepsilon)}+\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{2\varepsilon|s_{+}|(\lambda+\mu+\mathrm{i}2\rho)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,.\end{split}

(5.148)

Inserting this in (
5.147
) gives

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{+\infty}&b(\zeta,t;h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{h}\varphi(\zeta,t)}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=h^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{s_{+}(\sigma)}{|s_{+}(\sigma)|}\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}\,a(0,0)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}}{(\lambda+\mu+\mathrm{i}2\rho)^{\frac{1}{2}}}+{\mathcal{O}}(h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon)+{\mathcal{O}}(h)+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{2})\,.\end{split}

(5.149)

5.3
Summary of this section

Assume
\zeta\neq 0
. After a simultaneous rotation in
{\mathbb{C}}_{\zeta}
and
{\mathbb{C}}_{\xi+i\eta}
, (which amounts to changing the
family of contours
\Gamma_{\theta}
,) we may assume that
\zeta

does not belong to a conic neighborhood of
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}{\mathbb{R}}
in

{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}
. In Proposition
5.2
we have seen
that
\mathrm{II}(\zeta,1)=\underset{\delta\to 0}{\lim}\mathrm{II}(\zeta,1-\delta)
exists and is given by the second integral in
(
5.12
), rewritten in Proposition
5.5
as

\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}b(\zeta,t)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{|\zeta|^{2}}{h}\varphi(\sigma,t)}\,\mathrm{d}t
. Here
\widetilde{\zeta}(t,\zeta)
is given by
(
5.7
), where
|s_{+}|\geq 1/{\mathcal{O}}(1)
.

Set
\varepsilon:=\frac{|\zeta|}{\sqrt{h}}
. When
\varepsilon\geq h^{-\delta}
for some fixed
\delta>0
, we have a two term asymptotics expansion
of the original integral in Section
2
and the Stokes reduction
to
\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{II}+\mathrm{III}
is not needed. The
intermediate case is when
h^{\delta}\leq\varepsilon\leq h^{-\delta}

for some small and fixed
\delta>0
. In this case Proposition

5.7
gives an asymptotic formula for
\int_{T}^{+\infty}b(\zeta,t)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{|\zeta|^{2}}{h}\varphi(\sigma,t)}\,\mathrm{d}t
when

T\gg\max(1,\varepsilon^{-1})
, assuming that
\chi
in (
4.58
) has
sufficiently small support:
For
T\geq T_{0}\gg 1
,
T\gg\varepsilon^{-1}
, we have for every
b\in|\zeta|\mathbb{S}^{0}([0,+\infty[)
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
:

\displaystyle\int_{T}^{+\infty}b(t)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{|\zeta|^{2}}{h}\varphi(\sigma,t)}\,\mathrm{d}t=-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{|\zeta|^{2}}{h}\varphi(\sigma,T)}

\displaystyle\,\,\times

\displaystyle\left(c^{(N)}(T)+\mathcal{O}(1)\left(\frac{h}{(T|\zeta|)^{2}}\right)^{N+1}\frac{h}{T|\zeta|}\right)\,.

Here

c^{(N)}=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\Big(-\frac{h}{|\zeta|^{2}\partial_{t}\varphi}\partial_{t}\Big)^{k}\frac{h}{|\zeta|^{2}\partial_{t}\varphi}b=:\sum_{k=0}^{N}c_{k}\,.

We know that
\partial_{t}\varphi\asymp t
for large
t
and we can
view (
5.75
) as an asymptotic formula in increasing powers of

(T\varepsilon)^{-1}
.

We now turn to the case when
\varepsilon
is small in the sense
that
\varepsilon\leq h^{\delta}
for fixed small
\delta>0
. It
turns out that
\varepsilon
can be used as a small asymptotics
parameter and
\frac{1}{\varepsilon h^{1/2}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}b\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{h}\varphi(\zeta,t)}\,\mathrm{d}t
can first be developed as in (
5.107
), where "
b
" corresponds to our "
\frac{1}{\varepsilon h^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,b
" and further computations lead to (
5.143
),

\frac{1}{h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{+\infty}b(\zeta,t;h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{h}\varphi(\zeta,t)}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\left(h^{\frac{N}{2}-1}+\varepsilon^{2L}\right)+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)+\hbox{a linear
combination of terms}\\
\sum_{j=0}^{K}h^{\frac{j}{2}+\frac{\widetilde{K}}{2}-n}\varepsilon^{2\nu}\left(\sum_{\mu=0}^{j+\widetilde{K}}\varepsilon^{j+\widetilde{K}-\mu}\frac{\widetilde{d}_{\mu}}{\varepsilon}+\sum_{\mu=-M-1}^{-1}\varepsilon^{j+\widetilde{K}}\widetilde{d}_{\mu}\varepsilon^{-\mu-1}\ln(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\right)

with (
5.144
):

K,\widetilde{K},N,\nu\in\mathbb{N},\ \nu<L,\ \frac{\widetilde{K}}{2}-\left[\widetilde{K}/3\right]<\frac{N}{2}-1,\ n\leq\left[\widetilde{K}/3\right]\,.

Here
\widetilde{d}_{\mu}
also depends on
j,\widetilde{K},\nu
and

\sigma
.

This can also be written as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon\,}\int_{0}^{+\infty}b(\zeta,t;h)\,

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{h}\varphi(\zeta,t)}\,\mathrm{d}t={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\left(h^{\frac{N}{2}-1}+\varepsilon^{2L}\right)+\mathrm{an}(\varepsilon)+\hbox{a linear
combination}

\displaystyle\hbox{of terms }\varepsilon^{2\nu}h^{\frac{\widetilde{K}}{2}-n}\sum_{j=0}^{K}h^{\frac{j}{2}}\left(p_{j}(\varepsilon)\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{j+\widetilde{K}}q_{j}(\varepsilon)\ln(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\right)\,,

where
p_{j}
,
q_{j}
are polynomials in
\varepsilon
.
still with (
5.144
) valid.

From these considerations, we can also extract the leading term which
is given in (
5.149
):

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{+\infty}b(\zeta,t;h)\,&\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{h}\varphi(\zeta,t)}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=h^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{s_{+}(\sigma)}{|s_{+}(\sigma)|}\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}\,a(0,0)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}}{(\lambda+\mu+\mathrm{i}2\rho)^{\frac{1}{2}}}+{\mathcal{O}}(h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon)+{\mathcal{O}}(h)+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{2})\,.\end{split}

6
The term
\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1-\delta)
in (
3
)

A discussion of the third term in the decomposition
of Section
3
is presented in this section.

As in Proposition
3.5
we assume that
a
,
\phi
are
holomorphic in
\mathrm{neigh}((0,0);\mathbb{C}^{2})
and that
\chi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
has its support in such a neighborhood.

We start by looking at the deformation vector field associated to the
coordinates
w_{+}
,
w_{-}
, associated to the
\theta
-dependent basis

e_{+}
,
e_{-}
in Lemma
4.1
and in (
4.2
). Write
(
4.1
) as

\begin{split}&e_{+}=\left(\check{f}(\theta)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}},f(\theta)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\right)\in\Gamma_{\theta},\\
&e_{-}=\left(f(\theta)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}},\check{f}(\theta)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\right)\in\Gamma_{\theta},\end{split}

(6.1)

where

f(\theta)=\frac{1+\theta}{\sqrt{2(1+\theta^{2})}},\quad\check{f}(\theta):=f(-\theta)\,.

(6.2)

Then a calculation shows that

\partial_{\theta}f(\theta)=\frac{1}{1+\theta^{2}}\check{f}(\theta),\ \ \partial_{\theta}\check{f}(\theta)=-\frac{1}{1+\theta^{2}}f(\theta)\,.

(6.3)

(Note that
f(\theta)^{2}+\check{f}(\theta)^{2}=1
, so
f(\theta)=\sin\alpha
,
\check{f}(\theta)=\cos\alpha
, for a suitable
\alpha=\alpha(\theta)
.) A simple computation, using this and the fact that
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}=\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}
show that

\begin{cases}\dot{e}_{+}(\theta)=\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{1+\theta^{2}}e_{-}(\theta)\\
\dot{e}_{-}(\theta)=\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{1+\theta^{2}}e_{+}(\theta)\end{cases}\,,

(6.4)

where
\dot{e}_{\pm}=\partial_{\theta}e_{\pm}
.

The deformation vector field at a point

w_{+}e_{+}(\theta)+w_{-}e_{-}(\theta)\in\Gamma_{\theta}
is given by the
vector

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}:

\displaystyle=w_{+}\dot{e}_{+}+w_{-}\dot{e}_{-}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\theta^{2}}\big[w_{-}\,\mathrm{i}e_{+}+w_{+}\,\mathrm{i}e_{-}\big]\,.

If we identify the vector
\mathrm{i}e_{\pm}
with the real vector field

2\mathrm{Re}(\mathrm{i}\partial_{w_{\pm}})=\mathrm{i}\partial_{w_{\pm}}+\overline{\mathrm{i}\partial_{w_{\pm}}}=\mathrm{i}\partial_{w_{\pm}}-\mathrm{i}\partial_{\overline{w}_{\pm}}\,,

we obtain the deformation field

\widetilde{\nu}\simeq\frac{1}{1+\theta^{2}}\sum_{\bullet\in\{+,-\}}2\mathrm{Re}(\mathrm{i}\,w_{\widehat{\bullet}}\partial_{\bullet}),\hbox{ where }\widehat{+}:=-,\ \ \widehat{-}:=+\,.

(6.5)

Recall that the coordinates
w_{+},w_{-}
are real on
\Gamma_{\theta}.

We now turn to
\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1-\delta)
in (
3
)
which can be written

\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1-\delta)=\int_{0}^{1-\delta}k(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,,

(6.6)

where

\displaystyle k(\theta)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\Gamma_{\theta}}\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}

\displaystyle\times

(6.7)

\displaystyle\nu(\theta;\omega,\widetilde{\omega})

\displaystyle\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\left(\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\right)\,,

where
\nu
is given in (
3.24
). Now
\nu
and
\widetilde{\nu}
in (
6.5
) are both deformation fields associated to the
family
\Gamma_{\theta}
which however preserve different coordinates
on
\Gamma_{\theta}
. Thus
\nu-\widetilde{\nu}
is
tangential to each
\Gamma_{\theta}
and as explained before and in
Lemma
3.4
we can replace
\nu
in the expression for
J(\theta)
with
\widetilde{\nu}
and get

\displaystyle k(\theta)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\Gamma_{\theta}}\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\times

(6.8)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(\theta;\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\mathord{\rfloor}\mathrm{d}_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}\left(\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\right)\,.

Here we recall that
w_{\pm}
are defined as the coordinates on
\Gamma_{\theta}
in (
4.2
) and to each
\zeta
we associate the unique

\widetilde{\zeta}
with
(\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta})\in\Gamma_{\theta}
so that

(\zeta,\widetilde{\zeta})=z_{+}e_{+}+z_{-}e_{-},\quad z_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(6.9)

(This is different from (
4.8
)). Then as in (
4.4
)

z_{+}=\frac{\sqrt{2(1+\theta^{2})}}{1-\theta}\mathrm{Re}(\zeta\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}),\quad z_{-}=\frac{\sqrt{2(1+\theta^{2})}}{1+\theta}\mathrm{Re}(\zeta\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}})\,.

(6.10)

Using (
4.3
), (
6.10
) and (
4.7
), we obtain

\begin{split}k(\theta)=\frac{1}{\pi}&\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int\frac{a\big(\omega(w_{+},w_{-}),\widetilde{\omega}(w_{+},w_{-});h\big)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi\big(\omega(w_{+},w_{-}),\widetilde{\omega}(w_{+},w_{-})\big)}}{\dfrac{1-\theta}{\sqrt{2(1+\theta^{2})}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}(w_{+}-z_{+})+\dfrac{1+\theta}{\sqrt{2(1+\theta^{2})}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}(w_{-}-z_{-})}\times\\
\frac{1}{2(1+\theta^{2})}&\left(\sum_{\bullet\in\{+,-\}}2\mathrm{Re}(w_{\widehat{\bullet}}\,\mathrm{i}\partial_{w_{\bullet}})\right)\mathord{\rfloor}\left(\left(\partial_{\overline{w}_{+}}\chi)\mathrm{d}\overline{w}_{+}+(\partial_{\overline{w}_{-}}\chi)\mathrm{d}\overline{w}_{-}\right){\wedge}\mathrm{d}w_{+}\wedge\mathrm{d}w_{-}\right),\end{split}

which can be rewritten as

\begin{split}k(\theta)&=\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{2(1+\theta^{2})}}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{w_{+},w_{-}}}\hskip-25.0pt\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}a\big(\omega(w_{+},w_{-}),\widetilde{\omega}(w_{+},w_{-});h\big)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi\big(\omega(w_{+},w_{-}),\widetilde{\omega}(w_{+},w_{-})\big)}}{\big[(1-\theta)(w_{+}-z_{+})+\mathrm{i}(1+\theta)(w_{-}-z_{-})\big]}\times\\
{}&{}\\
&\big(\sum_{\bullet\in\{+,-\}}2\mathrm{Re}(w_{\widehat{\bullet}}\,\mathrm{i}\partial_{w_{\bullet}})\big)\mathord{\rfloor}\left((\partial_{\overline{w}_{+}}\chi)\mathrm{d}\overline{w}_{+}\wedge\mathrm{d}w_{+}\wedge\mathrm{d}w_{-}+(\partial_{\overline{w}_{-}}\chi)\mathrm{d}\overline{w}_{-}{\wedge}\mathrm{d}w_{+}\wedge\mathrm{d}w_{-}\right).\end{split}

(6.11)

This can be expanded explicitly, and for our estimates we retain that

\begin{split}k(\theta)=\mathcal{O}(1)\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{w_{+},w_{-}}}\hskip-20.0pt&\frac{\Big|a\big(\omega(w_{+},w_{-}),\widetilde{\omega}(w_{+},w_{-});h\big)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi\big(\omega(w_{+},w_{-}),\widetilde{\omega}(w_{+},w_{-})\big)}\Big|}{\big|(1-\theta)(w_{+}-z_{+})+\mathrm{i}(1+\theta)(w_{-}-z_{-})\big|}\times\\
&\times|\overline{\partial_{\omega,\tilde{\omega}}}\chi|\,\mathrm{d}w_{+}\mathrm{d}w_{-}\,.\end{split}

(6.12)

Here we recall that
\chi
denotes an almost holomorphic extension of
the original
\chi
on
\Gamma_{0}
. We can choose
\chi
so that

\overline{\partial}\chi
vanishes on

\mathrm{neigh}\big((0,0);\mathbb{C}^{2}\big)
. More precisely, we can
assume that
\overline{\partial}\chi
, viewed as a function on

\Gamma_{\theta}
, has its support in a set

K_{\theta}=([-b,b]\times[-b,b])\setminus([-a,a]\times[-a,a])\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{w_{+},w_{-}}\simeq\Gamma_{\theta}

(6.13)

where
0<a<b<\infty
are independent of
\theta
. The almost
holomorphicity tells us that

\overline{\partial}\chi=\mathcal{O}(\theta^{\infty})\quad\mathrm{on}\,\,\,K_{\theta}\,.

(6.14)

We assume that
\phi
satisfies (
4.23
), (
4.24
,
(
4.26
)) and (
4.27
). Then by (
4.31
), we have

\mathrm{Im}\,\phi_{2}\asymp\theta(|\xi|^{2}+|\eta|^{2})\hbox{ on }\Gamma_{\theta}\,.

Assuming that
\phi
is real valued on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\xi,\eta}
and
that we work in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
(0,0)\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
(where
\chi
has its support), we have

\mathrm{Im}\,\phi\asymp\theta(|\xi|^{2}+|\eta|^{2})\hbox{ on }\Gamma_{\theta}\cap\mathrm{supp\,}(\chi)\,.

(6.15)

In particular,

\mathrm{Im}\,\phi\asymp\theta\,\,\hbox{ on }\,\,K_{\theta}\,.

(6.16)

Decompose
K_{\theta}
into a disjoint union of rectangles
R
of
horizontal size (i.e. in the
w_{+}
-direction)
\asymp 1
and of
vertical size (i.e. in the
w_{-}
-direction)
\asymp 1-\theta
.
Let
(w_{+}(R),w_{-}(R))
be the center of
R
. Restrict the integral in
the right hand side of (
6.12
) to such a rectangle

R
:

k_{R}(\theta):=\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{R}\frac{\mathcal{O}(\theta^{\infty})\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\theta}{Ch}}}{\big|(1-\theta)(w_{+}-z_{+})+\mathrm{i}(1+\theta)(w_{-}-z_{-})\big|}\,\mathrm{d}w_{+}\mathrm{d}w_{-}\,.

(6.17)

Rescale in the vertical direction:

(1-\theta)\,\widetilde{z}_{-}:=(1+\theta)\,z_{-},\quad\mathrm{and}\quad(1-\theta)\,\widetilde{w}_{-}:=(1+\theta)\,w_{-}\,.

(6.18)

The corresponding points
(w_{+},\widetilde{w}_{-})
form a rectangle
\widetilde{R}
of horizontal and vertical size
\asymp 1
and with center

(w_{+}(R),\widetilde{w}_{-}(R))=(w_{+}(R),\frac{1+\theta}{1-\theta}{w}_{-}(R))
. We get

\begin{split}k_{R}(\theta)&=\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\widetilde{R}(R)}\frac{\mathcal{O}(\theta^{\infty})\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\theta}{Ch}}}{(1-\theta)\big|(w_{+}-z_{+})+\mathrm{i}(\widetilde{w}_{-}-\widetilde{z}_{-})\big|}\cdot\frac{1-\theta}{1+\theta}\,\mathrm{d}w_{+}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{w}_{-}\\
&=\frac{\mathcal{O}(\theta^{\infty})\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\theta}{Ch}}}{1+|z_{+}-w_{+}(R)|+|\widetilde{z}_{-}-\widetilde{w}_{-}(\widetilde{R})|}\,.\end{split}

(6.19)

Here
w_{+}(R),

\widetilde{\omega}_{-}(R)=\mathcal{O}\Big(\dfrac{1}{1-\theta}\Big)
.

Very roughly, the numbers

|{z}_{+}-{w}_{+}(R)|
and
|\widetilde{z}_{-}-\widetilde{w}_{-}(\widetilde{R})|
are distributed like the integers in an interval
[0,\simeq\mathcal{O}(1)]
,

[0,\simeq(1-\theta)^{-1}]
respectively, so when summing the
k_{R}(\theta)
over
the rectangles
R
in our decomposition, we get from (
6.19
):

k(\theta)=\mathcal{O}(\theta^{\infty})\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\theta}{Ch}}\sum_{k=0}^{(1-\theta)^{-1}}\frac{1}{1+k}\,.

(6.20)

The last sum in (
6.20
) is
\mathcal{O}(1)(1+\ln\frac{1}{1-\theta})
and we get from (
6.20
):

k(\theta)=\mathcal{O}(\theta^{\infty})\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\theta}{Ch}}\left(1+\ln\frac{1}{1-\theta}\right)\,.

(6.21)

Using this in (
6.6
) gives

\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1-\delta)={\mathcal{O}}(h^{\infty})\,.

(6.22)

We also see that

\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1-\delta)\longrightarrow\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1):=\int_{0}^{1}k(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta\in\mathbb{C}\,,

(6.23)

when
\delta\rightarrow 0
and
\mathrm{III}(\zeta,1)={\mathcal{O}}(h^{\infty})
.

7
Conclusion

In this paper, we have presented a detailed discussion of the
integral (
1.1
) for values of
0<h\ll 1
with a stationary
point of the phase
\phi(\omega)
at
\omega=0
, and this for a wide
range of values of the singularity
\zeta
. If
|\zeta|\gg\sqrt{h}
,
standard steepest descent techniques can be applied, but for

|\zeta|\leq\sqrt{h}
, this is no longer possible. We applied a
polarization approach to treat such cases, i.e. the problem is
considered on the anti-diagonal of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
with variables

\omega
,
\widetilde{\omega}
where
\widetilde{\omega}=\overline{\omega}
.
Here steepest descent contours were considered.
An application of Stokes’ theorem allowed for a decomposition of the to
be studied integral into three terms. For each of these terms the
dominant contributions for small
h
for various domains of
|\zeta|

were presented.

The current study was for the signature
(+,-)
of the Hessian matrix at
the stationary point. A numerical study of interesting cases of these
integrals appearing in the inverse scattering approach to the
Davey-Stewartson II equations shows that other possible signatures and
even degenerate cases are important in this context as well. The
application of the present approach to these cases will be the
subject of further research.

Appendix A
Review of wavefront sets for Remark
3.3

We here complete Remark
3.3
by
reviewing briefly some elements of the theory of wavefront sets. For
more details see e.g.
[
8
, Chapter 8, Section 8.2]
and also
[
7
, Chapter 7]
. If

X\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is open,
u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(X)
,

(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in X\times\big({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\big)
, we say that

(x_{0},\xi_{0})\not\in\mathrm{WF}(u)
if there exist

\phi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(X)
with
\phi(x_{0})\neq 0
and a conic
neighborhood
V
of
\xi_{0}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
such that

\widehat{\phi u}(\xi)={\mathcal{O}}(\langle\xi\rangle^{-\infty})
in

V
. Here
\widehat{\phi u}={\mathcal{F}}(\phi u)
denotes the Fourier
transform of
\phi u
, and we use the notation

\langle\xi\rangle=(1+|\xi|^{2})^{1/2}
. This defines a closed
conic subset
\mathrm{WF}(u)\subset X\times\big({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\big)
, and the
definition extends to the case when
X
is a smooth manifold where
now
\mathrm{WF}(u)\subset T^{*}X\setminus 0
with
0
here denoting
the zero section. If
\Omega
is a differential form, we define

\mathrm{WF}(\Omega)
as the intersection of the wavefront sets of
the coefficients, when expressing
\Omega
in local coordinates.

If
Y
is a closed submanifold of the manifold
X
with

\mathrm{dim}Y=k<n=\mathrm{dim}X
and
u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(X)
, then the
restriction
{{u}_{|}}_{Y}
is well defined in
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(Y)

provided that

\mathrm{WF}(u)\cap N^{*}Y=\emptyset\,,

(A.1)

where
N^{*}Y
denotes the conormal bundle of
Y\subset X
. Moreover,
under the assumption (
A.1
) we have

\mathrm{WF}\left({u}_{{|}_{Y}}\right)\subset\iota^{*}\Big(\mathrm{WF}(u)\cap T_{Y}^{*}X\Big)\,,

(A.2)

where
\iota^{*}
denotes the natural projection
T^{*}_{Y}X\to T^{*}Y
.

The term "well defined" can be given a precise sense using
approximation of distributions with smooth functions in semi-normed spaces adapted to wavefront sets.

We recall a simple proof of why
{u}_{{|}_{Y}}
is well defined
under the assumption (
A.1
). This is a local statement, and we are free to choose suitable local coordinates on
X
and on
Y
. We choose them near a point of
Y
of the form
x=(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})
,
x^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
,
x^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n-k}
, so that
Y
(locally near the given
point) becomes
x^{\prime\prime}=0
and the given point
x=0
.

Let
\phi\in C_{0}^{\infty}\big(\mathrm{neigh}(0,{\mathbb{R}}^{n})\big)
satisfy

\phi(0)\neq 0
. Then
N^{*}Y
becomes
x^{\prime\prime}=0
,
\xi^{\prime}=0
,
\xi^{\prime\prime}\neq 0
and (
A.1
) tells us that if
\mathrm{supp}\phi
is small enough, then

{\mathcal{F}}(\phi u)(\xi)={\mathcal{O}}(\langle\xi\rangle^{-\infty})\hbox{
when }|\xi^{\prime}|\leq|\xi|/C\,,

(A.3)

if
C>0
is large enough. By Fourier inversion
we have (formally to start with)

{\phi u}{{{}_{|}}_{Y}}(x^{\prime})=\phi u(x^{\prime},0)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int{\mathcal{F}}(\phi u)(\xi)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}x^{\prime}\cdot\xi^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\xi\,,

and hence if
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
denotes the Fourier transform on

{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
:

{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\left({\phi u}_{|_{Y}}\right)(\xi^{\prime})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n-k}}\int{\mathcal{F}}(\phi u)(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime\prime}\,

(A.4)

the integral converges for all
\xi^{\prime}
and gives a function of
temperate growth in
\xi^{\prime}
. This leads to the definition
{\phi u}_{|_{Y}}\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
and to
{u}_{|_{Y}}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
. Further easy estimates give (
A.2
).

Consider a smooth map
f:X\longrightarrow Y
, where
X
,
Y
are two open sets in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
,
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
respectively or more generally manifolds of dimension
k
,
n
.
Let
u
be a distribution on
Y
. To define

u\circ f
is equivalent to defining

({1_{X}\otimes u)}_{|_{\mathrm{graph}(f)}}
,
where
(1_{X}\otimes u)(x,y):=u(y)

and
\mathrm{graph}(f)=\big\{(x,f(x));\,x\in X\big\}
.
We check that

N^{*}_{(x,f(x))}\big(\mathrm{graph}(f)\big)=\big\{(\xi,\eta);\,\xi=-{}^{t}\hskip-2.0pt{f^{\prime}}(x)\eta\big\}\,,

(A.5)

\mathrm{WF}(1_{X}\otimes u)\subset\big\{(x,0;y,\eta);\,(y,\eta)\in\mathrm{WF}(u)\big\}\,,

(A.6)

hence

\begin{split}\mathrm{WF}&(1_{X}\otimes u)\cap N^{*}(\mathrm{graph}(f))\\
=&\Big\{(x,0;f(x),\eta);\,x\in X,\ (f(x),\eta)\in\mathrm{WF}(u),\ 0={}^{t}\hskip-2.0pt{f^{\prime}}(x)\eta\Big\}\,.\end{split}

(A.7)

In particular,

\mathrm{WF}\Big(1_{X}\otimes u\Big)\cap N^{*}\Big(\mathrm{graph}(f)\Big)=\emptyset\,,

(A.8)

if

{\mathcal{N}}\big({}^{t}\hskip-2.0pt{f^{\prime}(x)}\big)\cap\Big(\mathrm{WF}(u)\cap T^{*}_{f(y)}Y\Big)=\emptyset\,,\quad\forall x\in X\,.

(A.9)

Here
{\mathcal{N}}\big({}^{t}\hskip-2.0pt{f^{\prime}(x)}\big)
is the kernel of the linear transformation

\big[{}^{t}\hskip-2.0pt{f^{\prime}(x)}\big]
. See
[
8
, Theorem 8.2.4, page 263]
.

The map
\iota^{*}
can be identified with that of restriction of
differential 1-forms to the submanifold. When the submanifold is

\mathrm{graph}(f)
, and we identify 1-forms on that submanifold with 1-forms in
\mathrm{d}x
we get at a point
(x,f(x))
:

\iota^{*}(\xi\cdot\mathrm{d}x+\eta\cdot\mathrm{d}y)=\xi\cdot\mathrm{d}x+\eta\cdot f^{\prime}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\Big(\xi+{}^{t}\hskip-2.0pt{f^{\prime}}(x)\eta\Big)\cdot\mathrm{d}x\,.

(A.10)

We combine this with (
A.6
) and get

\mathrm{WF}\left({(1_{X}\otimes u)}_{|_{\mathrm{graph}(f)}}\right)\subset\Big\{(x,{}^{t}\hskip-2.0pt{f^{\prime}}(x)\eta);\,(f(x),\eta)\in\mathrm{WF}(u)\Big\}\,,

(A.11)

when (
A.9
) holds.

From the identification

{1_{X}\otimes u}_{|_{\mathrm{graph}(f)}}\simeq u\circ f
,
we get the equivalent inclusion

\mathrm{WF}(u\circ f)\subset\Big\{(x,{}^{t}\hskip-2.0pt{f^{\prime}}(x)\eta);\,(f(x),\eta)\in\mathrm{WF}(u)\Big\}\,,

(A.12)

when (
A.9
) holds.

Now consider a smooth map
f=f(t,x):\,I\times W\to Y\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
where
f(t,\cdot)
is injective with injective differential for every

t\in I
. Let
u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(Y)
and assume that
(
3.10
) holds with
\Gamma_{t}={\mathcal{R}}(f(t,\cdot))
. This
means that (
A.9
) holds for
f(t,\cdot)
, and
a fortiori it also holds for
f
. Then
u\circ f(t,\cdot)\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(W)
is well defined for every
t\in I
, and
u\circ f

is well defined in
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(I\times W)
.

We conclude from (
A.12
) that with
f=f(t,x)
as above,

\mathrm{WF}(u\circ f)\subset\Big\{(t,x;{}^{t}\hskip-2.0pt{(\partial_{t}f})\eta,{}^{t}\hskip-2.0pt{(\partial_{x}}f)\eta);\ (f(t,x),\eta)\in\mathrm{WF}(u)\Big\}\,.

(A.13)

Here
{}^{t}\hskip-2.0pt{\big(\partial_{x}f\big)}\eta\neq 0
, and we conclude that

(t,x;\tau,\xi)\in\mathrm{WF}\big(u\circ f\big)\,\Longrightarrow\,\xi\neq 0\,.

(A.14)

Restrict
t
to a compact sub-interval. We may decompose
u\circ f
by means of a partition of unity in the
x
variable. Let
\chi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(I\times W)
. We claim that

I(t):=\int\chi(t,x)(u\circ f)(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}x

(A.15)

is a smooth function of
t
. To prove this, let
\widetilde{\chi}(x)\in C_{0}^{\infty}
be equal to 1 on
\mathrm{supp}(\chi)
. Then

\begin{split}I(t)=&\int\widetilde{\chi}(x)(\chi u\circ f)(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
=&\int\widetilde{\chi}(x)\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int{\mathcal{F}}(\chi u\circ f)(\tau,\xi)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(t\tau+x\cdot\xi)}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}\xi}{(2\pi)^{k+1}}\,\mathrm{d}x\\
=&\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int({\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\widetilde{\chi})(-\xi){\mathcal{F}}(\chi u\circ f)(\tau,\xi)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t\tau}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}\xi}{(2\pi)^{k+1}}\,,\end{split}

(A.16)

where
{\mathcal{F}}
denotes the Fourier transform on
{\mathbb{R}}^{k+1}_{t,x}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
that on
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}_{x}
.

By (
A.14
) we have

{\mathcal{F}}(\chi u\circ f)(\tau,\xi)={\mathcal{O}}\Big(\langle(\tau,\xi)\rangle^{-\infty}\Big)\,,\ \hbox{ when }|\xi|\leq|(\tau,\xi)|/C\,,

(A.17)

for some large enough
C>0
. We split the last integral in
(
A.16
) into two terms by restricting the integration to
|\xi|\leq|(\tau,\xi)|/C
and
|\xi|>|(\tau,\xi)|/C
respectively. In
the domain of integration for the first term we have

({\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\widetilde{\chi})(-\xi){\mathcal{F}}(\chi u\circ f)(\tau,\xi)={\mathcal{O}}\Big(\langle(\tau,\xi)\rangle^{-\infty}\Big)\,.

In that of the second term we have
\langle\xi\rangle\asymp\langle(\tau,\xi)\rangle
, hence for some
N_{0}\geq 0
,

({\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\widetilde{\chi})(-\xi){\mathcal{F}}(\chi u\circ f)(\tau,\xi)={\mathcal{O}}(1)\langle\xi\rangle^{-\infty}\langle(\tau,\xi)\rangle^{N_{0}}={\mathcal{O}}(1)\langle(\tau,\xi)\rangle^{N_{0}-\infty}\,.

It follows that the last integral in (
A.16
) converges and is a
smooth function of
t
. Thus,

I(t)\in C^{\infty}\,.

(A.18)

This gives the smoothness of
\int_{\Gamma_{t}}\phi\Omega
in Remark
3.3
.

Appendix B
The special functions
G
, some complements.

In this appendix we collect some basic facts on the
special function
G
being the Hilbert transform of the Dawson
integral.

Let
Q(\omega)=q\,\dfrac{\omega^{2}}{2}
,
q\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. Assume

\mathrm{Im}Q(\omega)\asymp|\omega|^{2},\quad\omega\in\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\mathbb{R}\,.

(B.1)

Equivalently,
\mathrm{Re}\,q>0
. Recall that

G_{-iQ}^{l/r}(\omega)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{1}{\zeta-\omega}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}Q(\omega)}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,,

(B.2)

where
\Gamma
is a deformation of
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\mathbb{R}

oriented North East, avoiding
\zeta
with this point to the left/right.

Change of variable:
\omega=k\widehat{\omega}
, to obtain

\mathrm{i}Q(\omega)=-\frac{\widehat{\omega}^{2}}{2}\Leftrightarrow\mathrm{i}qk^{2}\widehat{\omega}^{2}=-\widehat{\omega}^{2}\Longleftrightarrow\mathrm{i}qk^{2}=-1\Leftrightarrow qk^{2}=\mathrm{i}\,.

(B.3)

Can take

k=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}}{\sqrt{q}}\,

(B.4)

with the branch of the square root satisfying
\arg({\sqrt{q}})\in]-\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{4}[
, so that
\arg({\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}})\in]-\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{4}[
. Then
\arg(k)\in]0,\frac{\pi}{2}[
.

Let
G^{l,r}=G^{l,r}_{\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}}
be as in
[
13
]
, modulo the multiplicative factor
2\pi\mathrm{i},
and with
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\mathbb{R}
replaced by
\mathbb{R}
. We get

\displaystyle G^{l,r}_{-\mathrm{i}Q}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{k^{-1}\Gamma}\frac{1}{\zeta-k\widehat{\omega}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\widehat{\omega}^{2}}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}(k\widehat{\omega})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{k^{-1}\Gamma}\frac{1}{\frac{1}{k}\zeta-\widehat{\omega}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\widehat{\omega}^{2}}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}(\widehat{\omega})

\displaystyle=G^{l,r}\Big(\frac{\zeta}{k}\Big)\,.

(B.5)

For the last equality, we see that
k^{-1}\Gamma
can be deformed to a small deformation of
\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle G^{l}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}G^{l}(\zeta+\mathrm{i}\varepsilon)=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\zeta+\mathrm{i}\varepsilon-\omega}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,,

\displaystyle G^{r}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}G^{r}(\zeta-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon)=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\zeta-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon-\omega}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,.

Here we encounter
\dfrac{1}{x\pm\mathrm{i}0}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
and
\dfrac{1}{x+\mathrm{i}0}+\dfrac{1}{x-\mathrm{i}0}=2\mathrm{v.p}\Big(\frac{1}{x}\Big)
,
so

\frac{1}{2}\Big(G^{l}(\zeta)+G^{r}(\zeta)\Big)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\Big[(\mathrm{v.p}\frac{1}{x})*(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}})\Big](\zeta)\,.

(B.6)

The distribution
\mathrm{v.p}\Big(\frac{1}{x}\Big)
is odd so
\frac{1}{2}\big(G^{l}+G^{r}\big)

is odd on
\mathbb{R}
and in particular
(G^{l}+G^{r})(0)=0
. Let us check the last fact directly:

G^{l}(0)+G^{r}(0)=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\underbrace{\Big(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\varepsilon-\omega}+\frac{1}{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon-\omega}\Big)}_{=:f(\omega,\varepsilon)}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,.

\begin{cases}f(-\omega,\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\varepsilon+\omega}+\frac{1}{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon+\omega}\\
{}\\
-f(\omega,\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\varepsilon+\omega}+\frac{1}{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon+\omega}\end{cases}\Longrightarrow f(-\omega,\varepsilon)=-f(\omega,\varepsilon)\,.

(B.7)

Thus
f(\cdot,\varepsilon)
is odd and hence
G^{l}(0)+G^{r}(0)=0
. This extends to
G^{l}_{-\mathrm{i}Q}(0)+G^{r}_{-\mathrm{i}Q}(0)=0
by (
B
). Moreover,

\begin{split}G^{r}(0)-G^{l}(0)&=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\,\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big(\frac{1}{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon-\omega}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\varepsilon-\omega}\Big)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\\
&=\,\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\frac{\varepsilon}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}}}{\omega^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\\
&=\,\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{2}}}{t^{2}+1}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{t^{2}+1}\,\mathrm{d}t=1\,.\end{split}

(B.8)

Then,

G^{r}(0)=-G^{l}(0)=\frac{1}{2}\,.

(B.9)

For more details concerning the properties of the special function
G
we can consult
[
1
, Chapter 7, page 295]
.

Appendix C
Invariance of
\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)
under rotations

In this appendix we study the invariance of the
studied integral under rotations of the steepest descent contours in
the polarization approach.

Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}\big(\mathrm{neigh}(0;{\mathbb{R}}^{2});{\mathbb{R}}\big)

with

\phi(0)=0\,,\,\,\phi^{\prime}(0)=0\,,\,\,\,\phi^{\prime\prime}(0)\hbox{ non-degenerate of
signature }(+,-)\,.

(C.1)

Let
\phi_{2}
be the quadratic Taylor polynomial of
\phi
at
0
,

\phi(\xi,\eta)=\phi_{2}(\xi,\eta)+{\mathcal{O}}((\xi,\eta)^{3})\,,\quad(\xi,\eta)\longrightarrow 0\,.

(C.2)

Write

\phi_{2}(\xi,\eta)=\frac{\lambda}{2}\xi^{2}-\frac{\mu}{2}\eta^{2}+\rho\xi\eta\,,\quad\lambda,\mu,\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(C.3)

Observe that

Lemma C.1
.

We have the following equivalences

\lambda>0\Longleftrightarrow\phi_{2}(\xi,0)\asymp\xi^{2}

(C.4)

and

\mu>0\Longleftrightarrow\phi_{2}(0,\eta)\asymp-\eta^{2}\,.

(C.5)

The set
S^{+}_{\phi_{2}}:=\phi_{2}^{-1}\big(]0,+\infty[\big)
is the
disjoint union of two convex mutually antipodal sectors and
S^{-}_{\phi_{2}}:=\phi_{2}^{-1}\big(]-\infty,0[\big)
has the same property.
The conditions (
C.4
), (
C.5
) amount to
\dot{\mathbb{R}}u_{1}\subset S^{+}_{\phi_{2}}
,
\dot{\mathbb{R}}u_{2}\subset S^{-}_{\phi_{2}}
, where
u_{1}=(1,0)
,
u_{2}=(0,1)
.

Let
\mathbf{U}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be a rotation of the form

\mathbf{U}={\mathcal{R}}_{\alpha}
as in (
C.15
) below. (
\mathbf{U}
is orthogonal with

\det\mathbf{U}=1
.) Let
\widetilde{\xi}
,
\widetilde{\eta}
be the
coordinates on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
for the orthonormal basis
(\mathbf{U}u_{1},\mathbf{U}u_{2})
,
so that

\xi\,u_{1}+\eta\,u_{2}=\widetilde{\xi}\,\mathbf{U}u_{1}+\widetilde{\eta}\,\mathbf{U}u_{2}\,.

Then

\phi_{2}=\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}}{2}\,{\widetilde{\xi}\,}^{2}-{\,}\frac{\widetilde{\mu}}{2}\,{\widetilde{\eta}\,}^{2}+\,\widetilde{\rho}\,\widetilde{\xi}\,\widetilde{\eta}\quad\hbox{ with }\,\widetilde{\lambda},\,\widetilde{\mu}>0\,,

if and only if

\mathbf{U}u_{1}\in S^{+}_{\phi_{2}}\,,\quad\mathbf{U}u_{2}\in S^{-}_{\phi_{2}}\,.

(C.6)

Assume that
\lambda,\,\mu>0
in(
C.3
) and let
\mathcal{C}_{+}
denote the component of
S^{+}_{\phi_{2}}
which contains
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\,u_{1}
. Identifying
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{\xi,\eta}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}_{\xi+i\eta}
, we have

\mathcal{C}_{+}=\big\{\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}\,;\,\gamma_{1}<\mathrm{arg}(\omega)<\gamma_{2}\big\}\,,

(C.7)

where

-\frac{\pi}{2}<\gamma_{1}<0<\gamma_{2}<\frac{\pi}{2}\,.

(C.8)

If
\mathbf{U}u_{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{+}
, then
\mathbf{U}u_{2}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2}}\mathbf{U}u_{1}
satisfies

\mathrm{arg}(\mathbf{U}u_{2})\in]\gamma_{1}+\frac{\pi}{2},\gamma_{2}+\frac{\pi}{2}[\,,

(C.9)

and
\mathbf{U}u_{2}\in S^{-}_{\phi_{2}}
if and only if
\mathrm{arg}(\mathbf{U}u_{2})\in]\gamma_{2},\gamma_{1}+\pi[
, i.e. if and only if

\mathrm{arg}(\mathbf{U}u_{1})\in]\gamma_{2}-\frac{\pi}{2},\gamma_{1}+\frac{\pi}{2}[\,.

(C.10)

We conclude that

\mathbf{U}u_{1}\in S^{+}_{\phi_{2}}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\mathbf{U}u_{2}\in S^{-}_{\phi_{2}}

(C.11)

if and only if

\mathrm{arg}(\mathbf{U}u_{1})\in\left]\max(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}-\frac{\pi}{2}),\min(\gamma_{2},\gamma_{1}+\frac{\pi}{2})\right[=:J\,.

(C.12)

The set of rotations
\mathbf{U}=\mathcal{R}_{\alpha}
satisfying (
C.12
) is
connected; the corresponding set of
\alpha
is the interval in
(
C.12
).

Under the assumption that

\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}{\mathbb{R}}\,,

(C.13)

we have that
I(\zeta):=\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)
is given by

I(\zeta)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\Gamma_{1}}\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\,,

(C.14)

where we recall that
\omega=\xi+i\eta
,
\widetilde{\omega}=\xi-i\eta
and
\Gamma_{1}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}{\mathbb{R}}_{\omega}\times\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}{\mathbb{R}}_{\widetilde{\omega}}
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\omega,\widetilde{\omega}}
. If
\zeta\neq 0
belongs to a small conic
neighborhood of
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\dot{{\mathbb{R}}}
, we shall use
\mathbf{U}=\mathcal{R}_{\alpha}

for a suitable
\alpha\in J
to achieve that a natural effective
\check{\zeta}
avoids
a conic neighborhood of
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\dot{{\mathbb{R}}}
.

In
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
we can identify
\mathcal{R}_{\alpha}
with

\mathcal{R}_{\alpha}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\alpha&-\sin\alpha\\
\sin\alpha&\cos\alpha\end{pmatrix}\,.

(C.15)

For
\mathbf{U}=\mathcal{R}_{\alpha}
with
\alpha
in the interval
J
in
(
C.12
), let
(\check{\xi},\check{\eta})
denote the linear
coordinates of a point
\rho=(\xi,\eta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
with respect
to the basis
(\mathbf{U}u_{1},\mathbf{U}u_{2})
. Then

\begin{pmatrix}\xi\\
\eta\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{R}_{\alpha}\begin{pmatrix}\check{\xi}\\
\check{\eta}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(C.16)

In analogy with the definition of
\omega,\,\widetilde{\omega}
,
we define
\check{\omega},\,\check{\widetilde{\omega}}
by

\check{\omega}=\check{\xi}+i\check{\eta}\,,\qquad\check{\widetilde{\omega}}=\check{\xi}-i\check{\eta}\,.

(C.17)

Then (
C.16
) gives

\omega=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\alpha}\,\check{\omega}\,,\qquad\widetilde{\omega}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\alpha}\,\check{\widetilde{\omega}}\,.

(C.18)

If
\check{a}
,
\check{\chi}
,
\check{\phi}
denote the functions

a
,
\chi
,
\phi
as functions of
(\check{\omega},\check{\widetilde{\omega}})
, so that

\check{a}(\check{\omega},\check{\widetilde{\omega}};h)=a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)

and similarly for
\chi
,
\phi
, and
\zeta\not\in\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\dot{{\mathbb{R}}}
, then

\begin{split}I(\zeta)&=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\check{\Gamma}}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\alpha}\check{\omega}-\zeta}\,\check{a}(\check{\omega},\check{\widetilde{\omega}};h)\check{\chi}(\check{\omega},\check{\widetilde{\omega}})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\check{\phi}(\check{\omega},\check{\widetilde{\omega}})}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\check{\widetilde{\omega}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\check{\omega}}{2\mathrm{i}}\\
&=-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\alpha}\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\check{\Gamma}}\,\frac{1}{\check{\omega}-\check{\zeta}}\,\check{a}(\check{\omega},\check{\widetilde{\omega}};h)\check{\chi}(\check{\omega},\check{\widetilde{\omega}})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\check{\phi}(\check{\omega},\check{\widetilde{\omega}})}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\check{\widetilde{\omega}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\check{\omega}}{2\mathrm{i}}\,,\end{split}

(C.19)

where
\check{\zeta}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\alpha}\zeta
and

\check{\Gamma}=\Big\{(\check{\omega},\check{\widetilde{\omega}})\in\mathbb{C}^{2};\,\,\,(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\alpha}\check{\omega},\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\alpha}\check{\widetilde{\omega}})\in\Gamma_{1}\Big\}\,.

(C.20)

Now drop the assumption that
\zeta\not\in\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\dot{{\mathbb{R}}}
and assume instead that

\check{\zeta}\mbox{ is outside a conic neighborhood of }\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}\dot{\mathbb{R}}\,\,\mathrm{in}\,\,\dot{\mathbb{C}}\,,

(C.21)

i.e.

\mathrm{arg}(\zeta)\not\in\mathrm{neigh}(\alpha+\frac{\pi}{4};{J})

(C.22)

and keep the assumption (
C.12
):

\alpha\in J\,.

(C.23)

We can then make the change of variables (
C.16
) in the
original integral in (
3.11
), (
3.14
) and get first as in (
C.19
)

\displaystyle I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\alpha}(\check{\xi}+\mathrm{i}\check{\eta})-\zeta}\,\check{a}(\check{\xi},\check{\eta};h)\check{\chi}(\check{\xi},\check{\eta})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\check{\phi}(\check{\xi},\check{\eta})}\,\mathrm{d}\check{\xi}\mathrm{d}\check{\eta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\alpha}}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\,\frac{1}{(\check{\xi}+\mathrm{i}\check{\eta})-\check{\zeta}}\,\check{a}(\check{\xi},\check{\eta};h)\check{\chi}(\check{\xi},\check{\eta})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\check{\phi}(\check{\xi},\check{\eta})}\,\mathrm{d}\check{\xi}\mathrm{d}\check{\eta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle:\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\alpha}\check{I}(\check{a}\check{\chi},\check{\phi},\check{\zeta};h)\,.

(C.24)

Then apply the results in Section
3
and the following ones to

\check{I}(\check{a}\check{\chi},\check{\phi},\check{\zeta};h)
. We get the
analogue of (
3
), (
3
):

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\alpha}I(a\chi,\phi,\zeta;h)=\check{I}(\check{a}\check{\chi},\check{\phi},\check{\zeta};h)=\check{\mathrm{I}}(\check{\zeta},1)+\check{\mathrm{II}}(\check{\zeta},1)+\check{\mathrm{III}}(\check{\zeta},1)

(C.25)

with the natural definitions of
\check{\mathrm{I}}
,
\check{\mathrm{II}}
,
\check{\mathrm{III}}
, analogous to those of

\mathrm{I}
,
\mathrm{II}
,
\mathrm{III}
. In particular,

\hskip-5.0pt\check{\mathrm{I}}(\check{\zeta},1)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{(\check{\omega},\check{\widetilde{\omega}})\in\Gamma_{1}}\,\frac{1}{\check{\omega}-\check{\zeta}}\,\check{a}(\check{\omega},\check{\widetilde{\omega}};h)\check{\chi}(\check{\omega},\check{\widetilde{\omega}})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\check{\phi}(\check{\omega},\check{\widetilde{\omega}})}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\check{\widetilde{\omega}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\check{\omega}}{2\mathrm{i}}\,,

(C.26)

which differs from the first integral in (
C.19
) only by the
choice of integration contour, cf. (
C.20
).

We can express (
C.26
) as an integral in the
\omega
,

\widetilde{\omega}
variables (cf. (
C.18
),
\zeta=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\alpha}\check{\zeta}
,

\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\alpha}\check{\mathrm{I}}(\zeta,1)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\Gamma_{\alpha}(1)}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}}{\omega-\zeta}\,a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\chi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\,\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}=:\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\zeta,1)\,,

(C.27)

where

\Gamma_{\alpha}(1)=\big\{(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\,;\,\,(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\alpha}\omega,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\alpha}\widetilde{\omega})\in\Gamma_{1}\big\}\,,

(C.28)

with
\Gamma_{0}(1)=\Gamma_{1}
.

Keeping the assumptions (
C.22
), (
C.23
), we
reinstate the assumption that
\zeta\not\in\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}{\mathbb{R}}
in
the strengthened variant

\mathrm{arg}(\zeta)\not\in\mathrm{neigh}(\frac{\pi}{4};J)\,,

and further strengthen the assumptions to

\mathrm{arg}(\zeta)\not\in\mathrm{neigh}(K_{\alpha};J)\,,

(C.29)

where we put

K_{\alpha}=[\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{4}+\alpha]\hbox{ when }\alpha>0,\,\hbox{ and }K_{\alpha}=[\frac{\pi}{4}+\alpha,\frac{\pi}{4}]\hbox{ when
}\alpha\leq 0\,.

(C.30)

Then
\mathrm{I}_{t\alpha}(\zeta,1)
in (
C.27
) is
well defined for
t\in[0,1]
, since
\omega-\zeta\neq 0
for

(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\in\Gamma_{t\alpha}(1)
.

Along
\Gamma_{t\alpha}(1)
we have
|\omega-\zeta|\geq|\zeta|/{\mathcal{O}}(1)
uniformly for
t\in[0,1]
. Moreover,
\mathrm{Im}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})\asymp|\omega|^{2}+|\widetilde{\omega}|^{2}
here, by (
4.63
), provided that we
work in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
(0,0)
. Assuming that

a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)
is holomorphic in

\mathrm{neigh}((0,0);\mathbb{C}^{2})
and that
\chi
has its support in such a
small neighborhood and equals to 1 in another similar neighborhood, we
can apply Stokes’ formula and get when
|\zeta|\ll 1

\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\zeta,1)-\mathrm{I}_{0}(\zeta,1)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\hskip-5.0pt\mathrm{d}t\int\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\Gamma_{t\alpha}(1)}\frac{1}{\omega-\zeta}\,a\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi}\,\nu\rfloor\frac{\mathrm{d}\chi\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega}{2\mathrm{i}}\,,

(C.31)

where
\nu
is a deformation field associated to our family of
contours. As before,
\mathrm{d}\chi\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega=\overline{\partial}\chi\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega
is supported away from
(0,0)
, hence

a(\omega,\widetilde{\omega};h)\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{h}\phi(\omega,\widetilde{\omega})}\,\nu\rfloor(\mathrm{d}\chi\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\omega}\wedge\mathrm{d}\omega)={\mathcal{O}}(1)\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{{\mathcal{O}}(h)}\Big)\,.

(C.32)

We use
(t\alpha)
-dependent coordinates as in
(
4.2
), to write

\Gamma_{1}\ni(\check{\omega},\check{\widetilde{\omega}})=w_{+}e_{+}+w_{-}e_{-}\,.

(C.33)

(
4.3
) gives
\check{\omega}=w_{-}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}}
, where
\omega=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}{t\alpha}}\check{\omega}
, hence
1/(\omega-\zeta)
in
(
C.31
) is

=\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}({t\alpha}+\frac{\pi}{4})}w_{-}-\zeta}=\frac{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}{|w_{-}|+|\zeta|}\,,

which produces a log contribution to the integral in (
C.31
)
and we get

\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\zeta,1)-\mathrm{I}_{0}(\zeta,1)={\mathcal{O}}(1)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{{\mathcal{O}}(h)}}\ln(\frac{1}{|\zeta|})\,.

(C.34)

The factor
\ln(1/|\zeta|)
in (
C.34
) can be dropped if

\ln(1/|\zeta|)\leq\mathrm{e}^{1/{\mathcal{O}}(h)}
i.e. if

|\zeta|\geq\exp(-\exp(1/{\mathcal{O}}(h)))\,.

(C.35)

Then, we obtain the following result

Proposition C.2
.

Let
\alpha\in J
where
J
is the interval in (
C.12
).
Assume that
\zeta
satisfies (
C.29
) and (
C.35
). Then

\mathrm{I}_{\alpha}(\zeta,1)=\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)+{\mathcal{O}}(1)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{{\mathcal{O}}(h)}}\,,

we recall that
\mathrm{I}(\zeta,1)
is the first term in the decomposition (
3
) (see also Proposition
4.2
), and
I_{\alpha}(\zeta,1)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\alpha}\check{\mathrm{I}}(\check{\zeta},1)
the corresponding term in (
C.25
), (
C.26
), (
C.27
).

Remark C.3
.

We have shown above that the asymptotics of the first term obtained via Stokes’ formula for the decomposition of integral (
C
) is invariant under rotations. The same arguments prove that the other two integrals,
\mathrm{II}
and
\mathrm{III},
obtained via the Stokes formula are also invariant under rotations.
